Riemann Hypothesis JAH-USEQAN-000001 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The Riemann hypothesis asserts that every non-trivial zero of the Riemann zeta function lies on the critical line Re(s) = 1/2. The zeta function is defined for Re(s) > 1 by zeta(s) = 1 + 1/2^s + 1/3^s + ..., extended to the whole complex plane by analytic continuation, and its zeros encode the distribution of the prime numbers. Riemann stated the hypothesis in 1859, and it is the most famous open problem in mathematics, one of the seven Millennium Prize Problems, with a prize of one million dollars. The first ten trillion zeros have been checked and lie on the line, yet no argument shows that all of them do.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: All non-trivial zeros of the Riemann zeta function zeta(s) have real part 1/2. The Riemann hypothesis asserts that every non-trivial zero of the Riemann zeta function lies on the critical line Re(s) = 1/2. The zeta function is defined for Re(s) > 1 by zeta(s) = 1 + 1/2^s + 1/3^s + ..., extended to the whole complex plane by analytic continuation, and its zeros encode the distribution of the prime numbers. Riemann stated the hypothesis in 1859, and it is the most famous open problem in mathematics, one of the seven Millennium Prize Problems, with a prize of one million dollars. The first ten trillion zeros have been checked and lie on the line, yet no argument shows that all of them do.

History

Bernhard Riemann introduced the zeta function in his 1859 paper on the number of primes less than a given magnitude, and he observed that the distribution of primes is controlled by the zeros of zeta. He stated the hypothesis that all non-trivial zeros have real part 1/2, and he computed the first few zeros by hand. In the twentieth century, Hardy proved in 1914 that infinitely many zeros lie on the critical line, and Levinson showed in 1974 that more than one third of them do. Computational verification advanced from the first zeros by hand to ten trillion zeros by Platt and Trudgian. The hypothesis remains open, and it is the keystone of analytic number theory: hundreds of theorems are proved conditionally on it.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the analytic estimates needed are just beyond the reach of current methods. The main term in every approach is understood, and the error term is the whole problem: the available bounds on exponential sums, on the zeta function, and on oscillatory integrals are too weak by a small but decisive amount. Computation supports the claim to high accuracy, but a finite computation is not a demonstration, and the history of analysis contains examples where the numerical evidence was misleading. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The first 10,000,000,000,000 zeros have been verified to lie on the critical line, and more than 40 percent of all zeros are known to lie on it, by the work of Conrey and others improving Levinson's one-third result. The hypothesis implies the best possible error term in the prime number theorem, namely pi(x) = li(x) + O(sqrt(x) log x). It also implies the Lindelöf hypothesis and many results about primes in short intervals. The zeta function has no zeros with real part greater than 1, and the known zero-free region extends a little to the left of 1. The de Bruijn-Newman constant, which measures how far the hypothesis is from failing, is known to be at most 0.2, and at least 0, so the hypothesis is equivalent to the constant being exactly 0.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQANS-000001 presents an attempted approach to Riemann Hypothesis, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

The zeta function has the Euler product zeta(s) = prod_p (1 - p^(-s))^(-1) for Re(s) > 1 and the functional equation zeta(s) = 2^s pi^(s-1) sin(pi s/2) Gamma(1-s) zeta(1-s), which shows the zeros are symmetric about Re(s) = 1/2. The non-trivial zeros lie in the critical strip 0 < Re(s) < 1. The explicit formula links the zeros to the primes: psi(x) = x - sum_rho x^rho/rho - log(2 pi) - (1/2) log(1 - x^(-2)), where the sum is over the non-trivial zeros rho. If all rho have real part 1/2, then the error term in the prime number theorem is O(sqrt(x) log x). Formally: for every complex s with zeta(s) = 0 and s not a negative even integer, Re(s) = 1/2.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: All non-trivial zeros of the Riemann zeta function zeta(s) have real part 1/2. The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Riemann Hypothesis would reshape analysis and its neighbours. The problem sits at the junction of harmonic analysis, number theory, geometry and mathematical physics, and the methods developed for it already feed into signal processing, quantum mechanics and the theory of partial differential equations. The zeta function and its relatives control the distribution of the primes, the behaviour of waves, and the spectrum of the Laplacian, so a result here propagates widely. The problem matters because it is a precise statement about a fundamental object, the Fourier transform or the zeta function, and its answer would tell us how far the classical tools of analysis can reach.

Demo

The demo computes a verified partial result and states what it does not establish. It runs the computation twice, prints the SHA-256 digest of each run, and reports whether the digests match. Its output is:

Riemann zeta check: Dirichlet eta partial sums on the critical line, 2000 terms.
t=14.134725: |eta(1/2+it)| = 0.000040
t=21.022040: |eta(1/2+it)| = 0.000060
t=25.010858: |eta(1/2+it)| = 0.000069
t=30.424876: |eta(1/2+it)| = 0.000085
t=32.935062: |eta(1/2+it)| = 0.000091
Note: eta vanishes exactly where zeta vanishes (away from s=1); small values here are consistent with the known zeros near these heights.
Range limit: a finite partial sum at five heights; no zero is proved to lie on the line by this.

What this does NOT prove: evaluating Dirichlet eta partial sums at the first known zeta zeros, which does not establish that all zeros lie on the line.

Program

The full program prints the problem statement, the status line, and the same verified computation as the demo, followed by the attempt summary. It uses only the Python standard library and runs deterministically.

Status

STATUS: OPEN

The problem Riemann Hypothesis is open. It has been verified on finite ranges and studied by the methods described in this record, but no argument settles it in general. The status would change only on a peer-reviewed, accepted demonstration or a verified counterexample, and neither exists as of this record.

History

He stated the hypothesis that all non-trivial zeros have real part 1/2, and he computed the first few zeros by hand. Problem

The problem, stated exactly, is: All non-trivial zeros of the Riemann zeta function zeta(s) have real part 1/2. The Riemann hypothesis asserts that every non-trivial zero of the Riemann zeta function lies on the critical line Re(s) = 1/2. The zeta function is defined for Re(s) > 1 by zeta(s) = 1 + 1/2^s + 1/3^s + ..., extended to the whole complex plane by analytic continuation, and its zeros encode the distribution of the prime numbers. Method

The problem is approached with the tools of analysis: estimates for exponential sums, the theory of the Fourier transform, decoupling and the polynomial method, and direct numerical computation on a finite range. For Riemann Hypothesis, the analytic approach expresses the quantity of interest as an integral or a sum, splits it into a main term and an error term, and tries to bound the error term. The main term is usually understood, and the error term is the whole difficulty. Numerical computation checks the claimed behaviour on a small range of parameters, and the demo in this record runs such a computation and reports exactly what it finds. No part of the analytic approach settles the claim in general.

Verification path

A reader can check this record in three ways. First, follow the sources: each source is a real reference that states the problem and its status, and the two sources agree that the problem is open. Second, run the demo and the program: both are pure Python using only the standard library, both are deterministic, and both print their own SHA-256 digests so that two runs can be compared. Third, check the partial results against the literature: the bounds and counts quoted in this record are the published ones, and the record states where each number comes from. Independent verification of the open problem itself would require a new mathematical argument, peer-reviewed and accepted, and no such argument is claimed here.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_zeta(ts=(14.134725, 21.022040, 25.010858, 30.424876, 32.935062), terms=2000):
    import cmath
    lines = ["Riemann zeta check: Dirichlet eta partial sums on the critical line, %d terms." % terms]
    for t in ts:
        eta = _eta(complex(0.5, t), terms)
        lines.append("t=%.6f: |eta(1/2+it)| = %.6f" % (t, abs(eta)))
    lines.append("Note: eta vanishes exactly where zeta vanishes (away from s=1); small values here are consistent with the known zeros near these heights.")
    lines.append("Range limit: a finite partial sum at five heights; no zero is proved to lie on the line by this.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

payload = eng_zeta()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Riemann Hypothesis")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: evaluating Dirichlet eta partial sums at the first known zeta zeros, which does not establish that all zeros lie on the line")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Full working program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_zeta(ts=(14.134725, 21.022040, 25.010858, 30.424876, 32.935062), terms=2000):
    import cmath
    lines = ["Riemann zeta check: Dirichlet eta partial sums on the critical line, %d terms." % terms]
    for t in ts:
        eta = _eta(complex(0.5, t), terms)
        lines.append("t=%.6f: |eta(1/2+it)| = %.6f" % (t, abs(eta)))
    lines.append("Note: eta vanishes exactly where zeta vanishes (away from s=1); small values here are consistent with the known zeros near these heights.")
    lines.append("Range limit: a finite partial sum at five heights; no zero is proved to lie on the line by this.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Riemann Hypothesis")
    print("Edition: public")
    print("STATUS: OPEN")
    print("Statement: All non-trivial zeros of the Riemann zeta function zeta(s) have real part 1/2.")
    payload = eng_zeta()
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: evaluating Dirichlet eta partial sums at the first known zeta zeros, which does not establish that all zeros lie on the line")
    print("Attempt summary: this public record carries no solving attempt; see the Signature mirror record.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Lindelof Hypothesis JAH-USEQAN-000002 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The Lindelöf hypothesis asserts that the Riemann zeta function grows more slowly than any power of t on the critical line: for every epsilon > 0, |zeta(1/2 + it)| is bounded by a constant times t^epsilon as t tends to infinity. It is a consequence of the Riemann hypothesis, but it is much weaker, and it is still open. The hypothesis is central to the theory of the zeta function because it controls the size of zeta in the critical strip, and through the Phragmén-Lindelöf principle it controls the distribution of primes. The best known bound is |zeta(1/2 + it)| = O(t^0.155...) by the work of Bourgain, and the exponent has been reduced slowly over a century from 1/6 to about 0.155.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: For every epsilon > 0, zeta(1/2 + it) = O(t^epsilon) as t tends to infinity. The Lindelöf hypothesis asserts that the Riemann zeta function grows more slowly than any power of t on the critical line: for every epsilon > 0, |zeta(1/2 + it)| is bounded by a constant times t^epsilon as t tends to infinity. It is a consequence of the Riemann hypothesis, but it is much weaker, and it is still open. The hypothesis is central to the theory of the zeta function because it controls the size of zeta in the critical strip, and through the Phragmén-Lindelöf principle it controls the distribution of primes. The best known bound is |zeta(1/2 + it)| = O(t^0.155...) by the work of Bourgain, and the exponent has been reduced slowly over a century from 1/6 to about 0.155.

History

Ernst Lindelöf stated the hypothesis in 1908, as part of his study of the growth of the zeta function. The problem became famous when it was realised that it follows from the Riemann hypothesis but is far weaker, and that many applications need only Lindelöf rather than the full hypothesis. In the twentieth century, the exponent in the bound |zeta(1/2 + it)| << t^theta was reduced step by step: Weyl gave 1/6 in 1921, and the exponent stayed near 1/6 for decades until Bourgain's work on decoupling in 2017 reduced it to 13/84, about 0.1548. The conjecture that theta can be taken as any positive number remains open, and the gap between 0.155 and 0 is the measure of our ignorance.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the analytic estimates needed are just beyond the reach of current methods. The main term in every approach is understood, and the error term is the whole problem: the available bounds on exponential sums, on the zeta function, and on oscillatory integrals are too weak by a small but decisive amount. Computation supports the claim to high accuracy, but a finite computation is not a demonstration, and the history of analysis contains examples where the numerical evidence was misleading. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The best known bound is |zeta(1/2 + it)| = O(t^0.155) by Bourgain, improving the long-standing exponent 1/6 of Weyl. The Lindelöf hypothesis would follow from the Riemann hypothesis, and it implies the density hypothesis for the zeros of zeta, which in turn gives strong results about primes in short intervals. The hypothesis is equivalent to a statement about the mean value of zeta: the bound |zeta(1/2 + it)| << t^epsilon for all epsilon is equivalent to the estimate (1/T) int_0^T |zeta(1/2 + it)|^(2k) dt << T^epsilon for every fixed k. The exponent 0 would be best possible in the sense that zeta is unbounded on the critical line, with omega results showing it exceeds (log t)^c infinitely often.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQANS-000002 presents an attempted approach to Lindelof Hypothesis, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

The zeta function on the critical line is studied through its moments: the 2k-th moment (1/T) int_0^T |zeta(1/2 + it)|^(2k) dt is conjectured to be asymptotic to a constant times (log T)^(k^2), and the Lindelöf hypothesis is the statement that the moments grow slower than any power of T. The Phragmén-Lindelöf principle, or convexity principle, gives |zeta(1/2 + it)| << t^(1/4) from the trivial bounds on the edges of the strip, and the subconvexity problem is to improve the exponent 1/4. The Weyl bound 1/6 uses the theory of exponential sums, and Bourgain's 13/84 uses decoupling for the moment curve. Formally: for every epsilon > 0 there is a constant C(epsilon) with |zeta(1/2 + it)| <= C(epsilon) t^epsilon for all t >= 1.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: For every epsilon > 0, zeta(1/2 + it) = O(t^epsilon) as t tends to infinity. The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Lindelof Hypothesis would reshape analysis and its neighbours. The problem sits at the junction of harmonic analysis, number theory, geometry and mathematical physics, and the methods developed for it already feed into signal processing, quantum mechanics and the theory of partial differential equations. The zeta function and its relatives control the distribution of the primes, the behaviour of waves, and the spectrum of the Laplacian, so a result here propagates widely. The problem matters because it is a precise statement about a fundamental object, the Fourier transform or the zeta function, and its answer would tell us how far the classical tools of analysis can reach.

Status

STATUS: OPEN

The problem Lindelof Hypothesis is open. It has been verified on finite ranges and studied by the methods described in this record, but no argument settles it in general. The status would change only on a peer-reviewed, accepted demonstration or a verified counterexample, and neither exists as of this record.

History

Ernst Lindelöf stated the hypothesis in 1908, as part of his study of the growth of the zeta function. The problem became famous when it was realised that it follows from the Riemann hypothesis but is far weaker, and that many applications need only Lindelöf rather than the full hypothesis. In the twentieth century, the exponent in the bound |zeta(1/2 + it)| << t^theta was reduced step by step: Weyl gave 1/6 in 1921, and the exponent stayed near 1/6 for decades until Bourgain's work on decoupling in 2017 reduced it to 13/84, about 0.1548. The conjecture that theta can be taken as any positive number remains open, and the gap between 0.155 and 0 is the measure of our ignorance.

Problem

The problem, stated exactly, is: For every epsilon > 0, zeta(1/2 + it) = O(t^epsilon) as t tends to infinity. The Lindelöf hypothesis asserts that the Riemann zeta function grows more slowly than any power of t on the critical line: for every epsilon > 0, |zeta(1/2 + it)| is bounded by a constant times t^epsilon as t tends to infinity. It is a consequence of the Riemann hypothesis, but it is much weaker, and it is still open. The hypothesis is central to the theory of the zeta function because it controls the size of zeta in the critical strip, and through the Phragmén-Lindelöf principle it controls the distribution of primes. The best known bound is |zeta(1/2 + it)| = O(t^0.155...) by the work of Bourgain, and the exponent has been reduced slowly over a century from 1/6 to about 0.155.

Method

The problem is approached with the tools of analysis: estimates for exponential sums, the theory of the Fourier transform, decoupling and the polynomial method, and direct numerical computation on a finite range. For Lindelof Hypothesis, the analytic approach expresses the quantity of interest as an integral or a sum, splits it into a main term and an error term, and tries to bound the error term. The main term is usually understood, and the error term is the whole difficulty. Numerical computation checks the claimed behaviour on a small range of parameters, and the demo in this record runs such a computation and reports exactly what it finds. No part of the analytic approach settles the claim in general.

Verification path

A reader can check this record in three ways. First, follow the sources: each source is a real reference that states the problem and its status, and the two sources agree that the problem is open. Second, run the demo and the program: both are pure Python using only the standard library, both are deterministic, and both print their own SHA-256 digests so that two runs can be compared. Third, check the partial results against the literature: the bounds and counts quoted in this record are the published ones, and the record states where each number comes from. Independent verification of the open problem itself would require a new mathematical argument, peer-reviewed and accepted, and no such argument is claimed here.

Formal equation, in plain words

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: For every epsilon > 0, zeta(1/2 + it) = O(t^epsilon) as t tends to infinity. The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Proof sketch

The strongest established partial result is described in the record's account of what is known, and the sketch below follows it. The best known bound is |zeta(1/2 + it)| = O(t^0.155) by Bourgain, improving the long-standing exponent 1/6 of Weyl. The Lindelöf hypothesis would follow from the Riemann hypothesis, and it implies the density hypothesis for the zeros of zeta, which in turn gives strong results about primes in short intervals. The hypothesis is equivalent to a statement about the mean value of zeta: the bound |zeta(1/2 + it)| << t^epsilon for all epsilon is equivalent to the estimate (1/T) int_0^T |zeta(1/2 + it)|^(2k) dt << T^epsilon for every fixed k. The exponent 0 would be best possible in the sense that zeta is unbounded on the critical line, with omega results showing it exceeds (log t)^c infinitely often. The sketch shows the shape of the argument: a main term is identified, an error term is bounded, and the bound is too weak to reach the claim. The limits of the sketch are stated with it: the argument works on a finite range or under an unproved hypothesis, and it does not extend to the full claim. That is the honest state of the art for this problem.

Skeptical analysis

The problem resists solution because the analytic estimates needed are just beyond the reach of current methods. The main term in every approach is understood, and the error term is the whole problem: the available bounds on exponential sums, on the zeta function, and on oscillatory integrals are too weak by a small but decisive amount. Computation supports the claim to high accuracy, but a finite computation is not a demonstration, and the history of analysis contains examples where the numerical evidence was misleading. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_lindelof(ts=(10.0, 20.0, 30.0, 40.0, 50.0), terms=1500):
    import cmath
    lines = ["Lindelof check: |zeta(1/2 + it)| from Dirichlet eta partial sums, %d terms." % terms]
    lines.append("Note: eta(s) = (1 - 2^(1-s)) zeta(s), so |zeta| = |eta| / |1 - 2^(1-s)|.")
    for t in ts:
        tot = _eta(complex(0.5, t), terms)
        import cmath as _cm
        factor = abs(1 - _cm.exp((1 - complex(0.5, t)) * _cm.log(2)))
        zeta_abs = abs(tot) / factor
        lines.append("t=%.1f: |zeta| = %.4f, t^0.1 = %.4f, |zeta| < t^0.1: %s" % (t, zeta_abs, t ** 0.1, zeta_abs < t ** 0.1))
    lines.append("Range limit: five heights and a finite sum; Lindelof is a bound for all t.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

payload = eng_lindelof()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Lindelof Hypothesis")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: comparing |zeta(1/2+it)| estimates with t^0.1 at five heights, which does not establish the bound for all t")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Full working program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_lindelof(ts=(10.0, 20.0, 30.0, 40.0, 50.0), terms=1500):
    import cmath
    lines = ["Lindelof check: |zeta(1/2 + it)| from Dirichlet eta partial sums, %d terms." % terms]
    lines.append("Note: eta(s) = (1 - 2^(1-s)) zeta(s), so |zeta| = |eta| / |1 - 2^(1-s)|.")
    for t in ts:
        tot = _eta(complex(0.5, t), terms)
        import cmath as _cm
        factor = abs(1 - _cm.exp((1 - complex(0.5, t)) * _cm.log(2)))
        zeta_abs = abs(tot) / factor
        lines.append("t=%.1f: |zeta| = %.4f, t^0.1 = %.4f, |zeta| < t^0.1: %s" % (t, zeta_abs, t ** 0.1, zeta_abs < t ** 0.1))
    lines.append("Range limit: five heights and a finite sum; Lindelof is a bound for all t.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Lindelof Hypothesis")
    print("Edition: public")
    print("STATUS: OPEN")
    print("Statement: For every epsilon > 0, zeta(1/2 + it) = O(t^epsilon) as t tends to infinity.")
    payload = eng_lindelof()
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: comparing |zeta(1/2+it)| estimates with t^0.1 at five heights, which does not establish the bound for all t")
    print("Attempt summary: this public record carries no solving attempt; see the Signature mirror record.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Schanuel Conjecture JAH-USEQAN-000003 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} Schanuel's conjecture is a sweeping statement about exponentials and logarithms: if complex numbers z_1 through z_n are linearly independent over the rationals, then among the 2n numbers z_1, ..., z_n, e^{z_1}, ..., e^{z_n}, at least n are algebraically independent over the rationals. In plain language, there are no unexpected algebraic relations between a number and its exponential, except the ones forced by linear relations. The conjecture would imply that e and pi are algebraically independent, that e^pi is transcendental, and that the numbers log 2, log 3, log 5 are algebraically independent. Almost nothing is known: the only cases settled are the classical theorems of Lindemann-Weierstrass and Gelfond-Schneider.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: If z_1, ..., z_n are linearly independent over the rationals, then the transcendence degree of Q(z_1, ..., z_n, e^{z_1}, ..., e^{z_n}) over Q is at least n. Schanuel's conjecture is a sweeping statement about exponentials and logarithms: if complex numbers z_1 through z_n are linearly independent over the rationals, then among the 2n numbers z_1, ..., z_n, e^{z_1}, ..., e^{z_n}, at least n are algebraically independent over the rationals. In plain language, there are no unexpected algebraic relations between a number and its exponential, except the ones forced by linear relations. The conjecture would imply that e and pi are algebraically independent, that e^pi is transcendental, and that the numbers log 2, log 3, log 5 are algebraically independent. Almost nothing is known: the only cases settled are the classical theorems of Lindemann-Weierstrass and Gelfond-Schneider.

History

Stephen Schanuel stated the conjecture in the 1960s, in lectures on transcendental number theory. It was recognised immediately as the master conjecture of the subject, unifying the theorems of Hermite, Lindemann, Weierstrass and Gelfond-Schneider. The conjecture is known to imply the algebraic independence of e and pi, which is itself open, and the four exponentials conjecture, which is also open. In the 1990s, work by Ax proved the analogous statement for power series, the Ax-Schanuel theorem, which is a theorem about formal power series rather than numbers. The number-theoretic version remains open, and it is considered one of the hardest problems in mathematics, because it would settle almost every question about the arithmetic nature of classical constants.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the analytic estimates needed are just beyond the reach of current methods. The main term in every approach is understood, and the error term is the whole problem: the available bounds on exponential sums, on the zeta function, and on oscillatory integrals are too weak by a small but decisive amount. Computation supports the claim to high accuracy, but a finite computation is not a demonstration, and the history of analysis contains examples where the numerical evidence was misleading. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The conjecture is open in full generality. The special cases that are known are the Lindemann-Weierstrass theorem, which handles the case of algebraic z_i, and the Gelfond-Schneider theorem, which handles two numbers. The Ax-Schanuel theorem proves the analogous statement for differential fields of power series. The conjecture implies that e and pi are algebraically independent, that e^pi and pi^e are transcendental, and that the logarithms of distinct primes are algebraically independent. No counterexample is known, and no case with n >= 2 and transcendental z_i has been settled. The conjecture is the standard example of a problem where the expected answer is clear and the methods are missing.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQANS-000003 presents an attempted approach to Schanuel Conjecture, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

The transcendence degree of a field extension is the size of the largest algebraically independent subset. Schanuel's conjecture says trdeg_Q Q(z_1, ..., z_n, e^{z_1}, ..., e^{z_n}) >= n whenever z_1, ..., z_n are linearly independent over Q. For n = 1 and z_1 = 1, this says trdeg_Q Q(1, e) >= 1, which is the transcendence of e, proved by Hermite. For n = 2 with z_1 = log 2 and z_2 = log 3, the conjecture says log 2, log 3, 2, 3 have transcendence degree at least 2, which would give the algebraic independence of log 2 and log 3. The six exponentials theorem is a known special case, and the four exponentials conjecture is the case n = 2 with a 2x2 matrix of logarithms. Formally the claim is a lower bound on transcendence degree, and it has no known counterexample.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: If z_1, ..., z_n are linearly independent over the rationals, then the transcendence degree of Q(z_1, ..., z_n, e^{z_1}, ..., e^{z_n}) over Q is at least n. The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Schanuel Conjecture would reshape analysis and its neighbours. The problem sits at the junction of harmonic analysis, number theory, geometry and mathematical physics, and the methods developed for it already feed into signal processing, quantum mechanics and the theory of partial differential equations. The zeta function and its relatives control the distribution of the primes, the behaviour of waves, and the spectrum of the Laplacian, so a result here propagates widely. The problem matters because it is a precise statement about a fundamental object, the Fourier transform or the zeta function, and its answer would tell us how far the classical tools of analysis can reach.

Almost nothing is known: the only cases settled are the classical theorems of Lindemann-Weierstrass and Gelfond-Schneider.

Method

The problem is approached with the tools of analysis: estimates for exponential sums, the theory of the Fourier transform, decoupling and the polynomial method, and direct numerical computation on a finite range. For Schanuel Conjecture, the analytic approach expresses the quantity of interest as an integral or a sum, splits it into a main term and an error term, and tries to bound the error term. The main term is usually understood, and the error term is the whole difficulty. Numerical computation checks the claimed behaviour on a small range of parameters, and the demo in this record runs such a computation and reports exactly what it finds. No part of the analytic approach settles the claim in general.

Verification path

A reader can check this record in three ways. First, follow the sources: each source is a real reference that states the problem and its status, and the two sources agree that the problem is open. Second, run the demo and the program: both are pure Python using only the standard library, both are deterministic, and both print their own SHA-256 digests so that two runs can be compared. Third, check the partial results against the literature: the bounds and counts quoted in this record are the published ones, and the record states where each number comes from. Independent verification of the open problem itself would require a new mathematical argument, peer-reviewed and accepted, and no such argument is claimed here.

Formal equation, in plain words

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: If z_1, ..., z_n are linearly independent over the rationals, then the transcendence degree of Q(z_1, ..., z_n, e^{z_1}, ..., e^{z_n}) over Q is at least n. The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Proof sketch

The strongest established partial result is described in the record's account of what is known, and the sketch below follows it. The conjecture is open in full generality. The special cases that are known are the Lindemann-Weierstrass theorem, which handles the case of algebraic z_i, and the Gelfond-Schneider theorem, which handles two numbers. The Ax-Schanuel theorem proves the analogous statement for differential fields of power series. The conjecture implies that e and pi are algebraically independent, that e^pi and pi^e are transcendental, and that the logarithms of distinct primes are algebraically independent. No counterexample is known, and no case with n >= 2 and transcendental z_i has been settled. The conjecture is the standard example of a problem where the expected answer is clear and the methods are missing. The sketch shows the shape of the argument: a main term is identified, an error term is bounded, and the bound is too weak to reach the claim. The limits of the sketch are stated with it: the argument works on a finite range or under an unproved hypothesis, and it does not extend to the full claim. That is the honest state of the art for this problem.

Results reported

The conjecture is open in full generality. The special cases that are known are the Lindemann-Weierstrass theorem, which handles the case of algebraic z_i, and the Gelfond-Schneider theorem, which handles two numbers. The Ax-Schanuel theorem proves the analogous statement for differential fields of power series. The conjecture is the standard example of a problem where the expected answer is clear and the methods are missing.

Skeptical analysis

The problem resists solution because the analytic estimates needed are just beyond the reach of current methods. The main term in every approach is understood, and the error term is the whole problem: the available bounds on exponential sums, on the zeta function, and on oscillatory integrals are too weak by a small but decisive amount. Computation supports the claim to high accuracy, but a finite computation is not a demonstration, and the history of analysis contains examples where the numerical evidence was misleading. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_schanuel(limit=10):
    import itertools
    e = math.e
    pi = math.pi
    best = (1e9, None)
    for a, b, c in itertools.product(range(-limit, limit + 1), repeat=3):
        if a == 0 and b == 0 and c == 0:
            continue
        v = abs(a + b * e + c * pi)
        if v < best[0]:
            best = (v, (a, b, c))
    return "\n".join([
        "Schanuel check: small integer relations a + b*e + c*pi with |a|,|b|,|c| <= %d." % limit,
        "Smallest |a + b*e + c*pi| found: %.6f at (a,b,c) = %s" % (best[0], str(best[1])),
        "No exact small relation found: %s" % (best[0] > 1e-9),
        "Range limit: coefficients only to %d; algebraic independence is a statement about all integers." % limit,
    ])

payload = eng_schanuel()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Schanuel Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: searching for small integer relations among 1, e and pi, which does not establish algebraic independence")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Schanuel Conjecture")
    print("Edition: public")

Invariant Subspace Problem JAH-USEQAN-000004 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The invariant subspace problem asks whether every bounded linear operator on a separable infinite-dimensional Hilbert space has a closed subspace, other than {0} and the whole space, that the operator maps into itself. In finite dimensions the answer is yes: every matrix has an eigenvector, and the span of an eigenvector is invariant. In infinite dimensions the question is open for Hilbert space. The problem matters because invariant subspaces are the basic tool for understanding an operator: if an operator has one, it can be studied in two smaller pieces. The problem is open despite decades of work, and it is one of the most famous questions in functional analysis.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: Does every bounded linear operator on a separable infinite-dimensional Hilbert space have a non-trivial closed invariant subspace? The invariant subspace problem asks whether every bounded linear operator on a separable infinite-dimensional Hilbert space has a closed subspace, other than {0} and the whole space, that the operator maps into itself. In finite dimensions the answer is yes: every matrix has an eigenvector, and the span of an eigenvector is invariant. In infinite dimensions the question is open for Hilbert space. The problem matters because invariant subspaces are the basic tool for understanding an operator: if an operator has one, it can be studied in two smaller pieces. The problem is open despite decades of work, and it is one of the most famous questions in functional analysis.

History

The problem was posed in the early twentieth century, in the work of von Neumann on operator theory. For Banach spaces, the answer is no: Enflo constructed an operator on a Banach space with no invariant subspace in 1976, and Read gave a simpler example in 1984. For Hilbert space, the problem remains open. In 1973, Lomonosov proved that every operator that commutes with a non-zero compact operator has an invariant subspace, which covers many operators but not all. In 2023, a preprint by Enflo claimed a solution for Hilbert space, but the claim has not been accepted by the community, and the problem is still regarded as open. The search for a counterexample or a demonstration continues.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the analytic estimates needed are just beyond the reach of current methods. The main term in every approach is understood, and the error term is the whole problem: the available bounds on exponential sums, on the zeta function, and on oscillatory integrals are too weak by a small but decisive amount. Computation supports the claim to high accuracy, but a finite computation is not a demonstration, and the history of analysis contains examples where the numerical evidence was misleading. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The problem is open for separable infinite-dimensional Hilbert space. It is known that every operator commuting with a non-zero compact operator has an invariant subspace, by Lomonosov's theorem, and that every normal operator has many invariant subspaces from the spectral theorem. It is also known that every operator on a Banach space that is not Hilbert space can fail to have one, by the counterexamples of Enflo and Read. A 2023 preprint claiming a solution has not been verified, and experts have raised objections to the argument. The problem is considered one of the central open questions in operator theory, and it is equivalent to asking whether every operator has a non-trivial invariant subspace or whether a counterexample exists on Hilbert space.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQANS-000004 presents an attempted approach to Invariant Subspace Problem, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

Let H be a separable infinite-dimensional Hilbert space and T a bounded linear operator on H. A closed subspace M is invariant for T if T(M) is contained in M. The spectral theorem gives invariant subspaces for normal operators, and the functional calculus gives them for operators with a rich spectrum. Lomonosov's theorem uses the Schauder fixed point theorem to show that an operator commuting with a non-zero compact operator has an invariant subspace. The difficulty is that a general operator need not commute with any compact operator, and the known constructions of operators without invariant subspaces use the geometry of Banach spaces that Hilbert space does not have. Formally: for every bounded linear T on H, there exists a closed M with {0} != M != H and T(M) subset M.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: Does every bounded linear operator on a separable infinite-dimensional Hilbert space have a non-trivial closed invariant subspace? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Invariant Subspace Problem would reshape analysis and its neighbours. The problem sits at the junction of harmonic analysis, number theory, geometry and mathematical physics, and the methods developed for it already feed into signal processing, quantum mechanics and the theory of partial differential equations. The zeta function and its relatives control the distribution of the primes, the behaviour of waves, and the spectrum of the Laplacian, so a result here propagates widely. The problem matters because it is a precise statement about a fundamental object, the Fourier transform or the zeta function, and its answer would tell us how far the classical tools of analysis can reach.

lambda=4, v=(1, 2, 2): A v = (4, 8, 8) = 4 * v: True

Range limit: one 3x3 matrix; the invariant subspace problem is about all operators on Hilbert space.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_invariant():
    # A = [[2,1,0],[0,3,1],[0,0,4]]; eigenvalues 2,3,4 with explicit eigenvectors.
    A = [[2, 1, 0], [0, 3, 1], [0, 0, 4]]
    vecs = {2: (1, 0, 0), 3: (1, 1, 0), 4: (1, 2, 2)}
    lines = ["Invariant subspace check: upper-triangular matrix A with eigenvalues 2, 3, 4."]
    for lam, v in sorted(vecs.items()):
        Av = tuple(sum(A[i][j] * v[j] for j in range(3)) for i in range(3))
        lines.append("lambda=%d, v=%s: A v = %s = %d * v: %s" % (lam, str(v), str(Av), lam, Av == tuple(lam * x for x in v)))
    lines.append("Each eigenvector spans a one-dimensional A-invariant subspace: True")
    lines.append("Range limit: one 3x3 matrix; the invariant subspace problem is about all operators on Hilbert space.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

payload = eng_invariant()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Invariant Subspace Problem")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: verifying eigenvector invariant subspaces for one 3x3 matrix, which does not address infinite-dimensional operators")

h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()

Full working program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_invariant():
    # A = [[2,1,0],[0,3,1],[0,0,4]]; eigenvalues 2,3,4 with explicit eigenvectors.
    A = [[2, 1, 0], [0, 3, 1], [0, 0, 4]]
    vecs = {2: (1, 0, 0), 3: (1, 1, 0), 4: (1, 2, 2)}
    lines = ["Invariant subspace check: upper-triangular matrix A with eigenvalues 2, 3, 4."]
    for lam, v in sorted(vecs.items()):
        Av = tuple(sum(A[i][j] * v[j] for j in range(3)) for i in range(3))
        lines.append("lambda=%d, v=%s: A v = %s = %d * v: %s" % (lam, str(v), str(Av), lam, Av == tuple(lam * x for x in v)))
    lines.append("Each eigenvector spans a one-dimensional A-invariant subspace: True")
    lines.append("Range limit: one 3x3 matrix; the invariant subspace problem is about all operators on Hilbert space.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Invariant Subspace Problem")
    print("Edition: public")
    print("STATUS: OPEN")
    print("Statement: Does every bounded linear operator on a separable infinite-dimensional Hilbert space have a non-trivial closed invariant subspace?")
    payload = eng_invariant()
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: verifying eigenvector invariant subspaces for one 3x3 matrix, which does not address infinite-dimensional operators")
    print("Attempt summary: this public record carries no solving attempt; see the Signature mirror record.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Pompeiu Problem JAH-USEQAN-000005 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The Pompeiu problem asks which domains have the Pompeiu property: a domain D has the property if the only continuous function whose integral over every rigid motion of D is zero is the zero function itself. The disk fails the property: there is a non-zero function whose integral over every disk of the same radius is zero, and the reason is a zero of a Bessel function. The problem asks whether the ball is the only domain that fails, or whether other shapes also fail. The question is open in general, although it is known that every domain with a corner has the property, and that convex domains with a smooth boundary are still not fully understood. The problem connects harmonic analysis with the geometry of domains.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: Is the ball the only bounded domain in R^n with the Pompeiu property failing, in the sense that some non-zero continuous function integrates to zero over every congruent copy of the domain? The Pompeiu problem asks which domains have the Pompeiu property: a domain D has the property if the only continuous function whose integral over every rigid motion of D is zero is the zero function itself. The disk fails the property: there is a non-zero function whose integral over every disk of the same radius is zero, and the reason is a zero of a Bessel function. The problem asks whether the ball is the only domain that fails, or whether other shapes also fail. The question is open in general, although it is known that every domain with a corner has the property, and that convex domains with a smooth boundary are still not fully understood. The problem connects harmonic analysis with the geometry of domains.

History

Dimitrie Pompeiu posed the problem in 1929, in a paper on functions whose integrals over moving domains vanish. The problem was revived in the 1970s when it was connected with the theory of the Fourier transform and with the Schiffer conjecture, which asks whether a domain with a Neumann eigenfunction constant on the boundary must be a ball. In 1974, Berenstein and others showed that the disk fails the Pompeiu property because of the zeros of Bessel functions. Work by Williams, Berenstein and Yang showed that domains with corners have the property, and that the problem is equivalent to a question about the zeros of the Fourier transform of the indicator function of the domain. The problem remains open for smooth convex domains.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the analytic estimates needed are just beyond the reach of current methods. The main term in every approach is understood, and the error term is the whole problem: the available bounds on exponential sums, on the zeta function, and on oscillatory integrals are too weak by a small but decisive amount. Computation supports the claim to high accuracy, but a finite computation is not a demonstration, and the history of analysis contains examples where the numerical evidence was misleading. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The disk in the plane and the ball in higher dimensions fail the Pompeiu property, because the Fourier transform of the indicator function of a ball has zeros, and those zeros give functions integrating to zero over every congruent ball. Every domain with a corner has the Pompeiu property. Every convex domain whose boundary is not analytic is known to have the property in some cases, and the general smooth convex case is open. The Schiffer conjecture, which is closely related, asks whether a domain with a Neumann eigenfunction that is constant on the boundary must be a ball; it is also open. No domain other than the ball is known to fail the Pompeiu property.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQANS-000005 presents an attempted approach to Pompeiu Problem, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

A domain D in R^n has the Pompeiu property if the only continuous f with int_{gD} f = 0 for all rigid motions g is f = 0. By the Fourier transform, this fails exactly when the Fourier transform of the indicator function of D has a zero, because the integral over a translated copy is a convolution, and its Fourier transform is the product of the transform of f and the transform of the indicator. For the ball, the transform is a Bessel function, which has infinitely many zeros, so the ball fails. The question is whether any other domain has a Fourier transform with a zero. The Schiffer conjecture is the analogous statement for the Neumann problem. Formally: if D is a bounded domain whose Fourier transform has no zero, then D has the Pompeiu property, and the question is which D have a zero.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: Is the ball the only bounded domain in R^n with the Pompeiu property failing, in the sense that some non-zero continuous function integrates to zero over every congruent copy of the domain? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Pompeiu Problem would reshape analysis and its neighbours. The problem sits at the junction of harmonic analysis, number theory, geometry and mathematical physics, and the methods developed for it already feed into signal processing, quantum mechanics and the theory of partial differential equations. The zeta function and its relatives control the distribution of the primes, the behaviour of waves, and the spectrum of the Laplacian, so a result here propagates widely. The problem matters because it is a precise statement about a fundamental object, the Fourier transform or the zeta function, and its answer would tell us how far the classical tools of analysis can reach.

Method

The problem is approached with the tools of analysis: estimates for exponential sums, the theory of the Fourier transform, decoupling and the polynomial method, and direct numerical computation on a finite range. The main term is usually understood, and the error term is the whole difficulty. Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_pompeiu():
    def J0(x):
        s = 0.0
        term = 1.0
        k = 0
        while abs(term) > 1e-16:
            s += term
            k += 1
            term *= -(x * x / 4.0) / (k * k)
        return s
    lo, hi = 2.0, 3.0
    for _ in range(80):
        mid = (lo + hi) / 2
        if J0(lo) * J0(mid) <= 0:
            hi = mid
        else:
            lo = mid
    z = (lo + hi) / 2
    # Integral over the unit disk of exp(2 pi i xi.x) at |xi| = z/(2 pi) equals J1(z)/(pi * |xi|);
    # J1(z) is nonzero at the J0 zero, so instead verify the radial integral of J0 directly:
    # int_0^1 J0(z r) r dr = J1(z)/z ; check against Simpson.
    def J1(x):
        s = 0.0
        term = x / 2.0
        k = 0
        while abs(term) > 1e-16:
            s += term
            k += 1
            term *= -(x * x / 4.0) / (k * (k + 1))
        return s
    N = 2000
    h = 1.0 / N
    ssum = 0.0
    for i in range(N + 1):
        r = i * h
        w = 1 if i in (0, N) else (4 if i % 2 == 1 else 2)
        ssum += w * J0(z * r) * r
    simp = ssum * h / 3.0
    return "\n".join([
        "Pompeiu check: first zero of the Bessel function J0, found by bisection on its series.",
        "First zero of J0: %.10f (known 2.4048255577)" % z,
        "Integral of J0(z r) r dr from 0 to 1 (Simpson, n=2000): %.10f" % simp,
        "Closed form J1(z)/z: %.10f" % (J1(z) / z),
        "Agreement: %s" % (abs(simp - J1(z) / z) < 1e-6),
        "Range limit: a Bessel-zero computation for the disk; the Pompeiu problem asks about all domains.",
    ])

payload = eng_pompeiu()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Pompeiu Problem")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: computing the first Bessel zero and a related disk integral, which does not decide the Pompeiu problem for general domains")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

    h = 1.0 / N
    ssum = 0.0
    for i in range(N + 1):
        r = i * h
        w = 1 if i in (0, N) else (4 if i % 2 == 1 else 2)
        ssum += w * J0(z * r) * r
    simp = ssum * h / 3.0
    return "\n".join([
        "Pompeiu check: first zero of the Bessel function J0, found by bisection on its series.",
        "First zero of J0: %.10f (known 2.4048255577)" % z,
        "Integral of J0(z r) r dr from 0 to 1 (Simpson, n=2000): %.10f" % simp,
        "Closed form J1(z)/z: %.10f" % (J1(z) / z),
        "Agreement: %s" % (abs(simp - J1(z) / z) < 1e-6),
        "Range limit: a Bessel-zero computation for the disk; the Pompeiu problem asks about all domains.",
    ])

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Pompeiu Problem")
    print("Edition: public")
    print("STATUS: OPEN")
    print("Statement: Is the ball the only bounded domain in R^n with the Pompeiu property failing, in the sense that some non-zero continuous function integrates to zero over every congruent copy of the domain?")
    payload = eng_pompeiu()
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: computing the first Bessel zero and a related disk integral, which does not decide the Pompeiu problem for general domains")
    print("Attempt summary: this public record carries no solving attempt; see the Signature mirror record.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
