Fuglede Conjecture in Dimensions 1 and 2 JAH-USEQAN-000006 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} Fuglede's conjecture, proposed by Bent Fuglede in 1974, states that a domain in R^d is a spectral set if and only if it tiles space by translation. A spectral set is one for which the exponentials e^{2 pi i lambda . x} form an orthogonal basis of L^2 of the domain for some set Lambda of frequencies; a tile is a set whose translates by some discrete set cover space without overlap. The conjecture is now known to be false in dimensions 3 and above: Tao showed it fails in dimension 5 and higher in 2004, and later work showed it fails in dimensions 3 and 4. The remaining open case is dimensions 1 and 2, where the conjecture might still be true. This record covers that remaining open case.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: In R^d for d = 1, 2, is a domain a spectral set if and only if it tiles space by translation? Fuglede's conjecture, proposed by Bent Fuglede in 1974, states that a domain in R^d is a spectral set if and only if it tiles space by translation. A spectral set is one for which the exponentials e^{2 pi i lambda . x} form an orthogonal basis of L^2 of the domain for some set Lambda of frequencies; a tile is a set whose translates by some discrete set cover space without overlap. The conjecture is now known to be false in dimensions 3 and above: Tao showed it fails in dimension 5 and higher in 2004, and later work showed it fails in dimensions 3 and 4. The remaining open case is dimensions 1 and 2, where the conjecture might still be true. This record covers that remaining open case.

History

Fuglede proved the conjecture in 1974 for fundamental domains of lattices, and the problem became a central question in harmonic analysis. In 2003, Iosevich, Katz and Tao proved it for convex planar domains. In 2004, Tao constructed a counterexample in dimension 5 and above, using a finite set in a finite abelian group lifted to Euclidean space, and the construction was extended to dimensions 3 and 4 by Farkas, Matolcsi and others. In 2019, Lev and Matolcsi proved the conjecture for all convex domains in all dimensions. The cases of dimension 1 and 2 for general domains remain open, and they are the subject of active research, with the one-dimensional case reduced to questions about finite sets of integers.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the analytic estimates needed are just beyond the reach of current methods. The main term in every approach is understood, and the error term is the whole problem: the available bounds on exponential sums, on the zeta function, and on oscillatory integrals are too weak by a small but decisive amount. Computation supports the claim to high accuracy, but a finite computation is not a demonstration, and the history of analysis contains examples where the numerical evidence was misleading. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The conjecture is false in dimensions 3, 4 and 5 and above, and true for convex domains in all dimensions. In dimension 1, the conjecture is equivalent to a statement about finite subsets of the integers, and it is known for sets of size up to 6 and for many special cases. In dimension 2, it is known for convex domains and for some other classes, but not in general. The finite-group version of the conjecture has been studied extensively, and counterexamples are known in some groups while the conjecture holds in others. The one-dimensional case is connected with the theory of tilings of the line and with the Coven-Meyerowitz conjecture on tilings of the integers.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQANS-000006 presents an attempted approach to Fuglede Conjecture in Dimensions 1 and 2, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

A set Omega of positive finite measure is spectral if there is a set Lambda such that {e^{2 pi i }}_{lambda in Lambda} is an orthogonal basis of L^2(Omega), and it is a tile if there is a discrete set T with the translates Omega + t covering R^d almost disjointly. The conjecture is the equivalence of these two properties. In dimension 1, a set is spectral if and only if a certain trigonometric polynomial has no zeros, and the tiling condition is a statement about the zeros of the mask polynomial of a finite set. The counterexample in higher dimensions uses a set in Z_p^d that tiles but has no spectrum, or vice versa, and lifts it to R^d by a standard construction. Formally, for d = 1, 2: Omega is spectral if and only if Omega tiles R^d by translation.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: In R^d for d = 1, 2, is a domain a spectral set if and only if it tiles space by translation? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Fuglede Conjecture in Dimensions 1 and 2 would reshape analysis and its neighbours. The problem sits at the junction of harmonic analysis, number theory, geometry and mathematical physics, and the methods developed for it already feed into signal processing, quantum mechanics and the theory of partial differential equations. The zeta function and its relatives control the distribution of the primes, the behaviour of waves, and the spectrum of the Laplacian, so a result here propagates widely. The problem matters because it is a precise statement about a fundamental object, the Fourier transform or the zeta function, and its answer would tell us how far the classical tools of analysis can reach.

Demo

The demo computes a verified partial result and states what it does not establish. It runs the computation twice, prints the SHA-256 digest of each run, and reports whether the digests match. Its output is:

Subsets of size 2: 9 tile, 9 spectral, 9 both, tile<->spectral holds: True
Subsets of size 3: 8 tile, 8 spectral, 8 both, tile<->spectral holds: True
Range limit: the cyclic group Z/6 only; Fuglede in R^d for d = 1, 2 is open.

What this does NOT prove: verifying that tiling and spectrality agree for all subsets of Z/6, which does not establish the conjecture in dimensions 1 and 2.

Program

The full program prints the problem statement, the status line, and the same verified computation as the demo, followed by the attempt summary. It uses only the Python standard library and runs deterministically.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_fuglede_z6():
    import cmath
    N = 6
    def is_tile(A):
        for shift in range(N):
            pass
        # A tiles Z_N if some T with |A|*|T| = N partitions the group
        from itertools import combinations
        others = [i for i in range(N) if i not in A]
        for tsize in range(1, N + 1):
            if len(A) * tsize != N:
                continue
            for T in combinations(range(N), tsize):
                covered = []
                for a in A:
                    for t in T:
                        covered.append((a + t) % N)
                if sorted(covered) == list(range(N)):
                    return True
        return False
    def is_spectral(A):
        from itertools import combinations
        for lam in combinations(range(N), len(A)):
            ok = True
            for i in range(len(lam)):
                for j in range(i + 1, len(lam)):
                    d = (lam[i] - lam[j]) % N
                    s = sum(cmath.exp(2j * math.pi * d * a / N) for a in A)
                    if abs(s) > 1e-9:
                        ok = False
                        break
                if not ok:
                    break
            if ok:
                return True
        return False
    lines = ["Fuglede check: subsets of Z/6 tested for tiling and for being spectral."]
    for size in (2, 3):
        from itertools import combinations
        tile_n = spec_n = both_n = 0
        for A in combinations(range(N), size):
            t, s = is_tile(set(A)), is_spectral(set(A))
            tile_n += t
            spec_n += s
            both_n += (t and s)
            if t != s:
                lines.append("MISMATCH subset %s: tile=%s spectral=%s" % (str(A), t, s))
        lines.append("Subsets of size %d: %d tile, %d spectral, %d both, tile<->spectral holds: %s"
                     % (size, tile_n, spec_n, both_n, tile_n == spec_n == both_n))
    lines.append("Range limit: the cyclic group Z/6 only; Fuglede in R^d for d = 1, 2 is open.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

payload = eng_fuglede_z6()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Fuglede Conjecture in Dimensions 1 and 2")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: verifying that tiling and spectrality agree for all subsets of Z/6, which does not establish the conjecture in dimensions 1 and 2")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Subsets of size 3: 8 tile, 8 spectral, 8 both, tile<->spectral holds: True

Run 2 SHA-256: e2c518051b45723c5adfc87c000042a735c066937bec39ca464379c0702d54c3

Kakeya Set Conjecture JAH-USEQAN-000007 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The Kakeya set conjecture, also called the Kakeya conjecture, asserts that a Kakeya set in R^n has Hausdorff dimension n. A Kakeya set is a set that contains a unit line segment in every direction: for each direction, there is a segment of length 1 pointing that way somewhere in the set. In the plane, Besicovitch showed in 1919 that Kakeya sets can have area zero, so they can be very small in measure, but Davies proved in 1971 that every planar Kakeya set has Hausdorff dimension 2. In higher dimensions the conjecture is open: the best known lower bound for the dimension of a Kakeya set in R^n is about n/2 + something, far from n. The conjecture is a central problem in harmonic analysis and is implied by the restriction and local smoothing conjectures.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: Does every Kakeya set in R^n have Hausdorff dimension n? Equivalently, does a set containing a unit line segment in every direction have full dimension? The Kakeya set conjecture, also called the Kakeya conjecture, asserts that a Kakeya set in R^n has Hausdorff dimension n. A Kakeya set is a set that contains a unit line segment in every direction: for each direction, there is a segment of length 1 pointing that way somewhere in the set. In the plane, Besicovitch showed in 1919 that Kakeya sets can have area zero, so they can be very small in measure, but Davies proved in 1971 that every planar Kakeya set has Hausdorff dimension 2. In higher dimensions the conjecture is open: the best known lower bound for the dimension of a Kakeya set in R^n is about n/2 + something, far from n. The conjecture is a central problem in harmonic analysis and is implied by the restriction and local smoothing conjectures.

History

The Kakeya problem began with the question of the smallest area in which a needle of length 1 can be turned around, posed by Kakeya in 1917. Besicovitch showed the area can be made arbitrarily small, which surprised everyone. The modern form, about Hausdorff dimension, was developed by Fefferman and others in the 1970s, when the Kakeya problem was connected with the restriction problem for the Fourier transform and with the Bochner-Riesz conjecture. In 1999, Wolff proved the dimension is at least (n+2)/2, and in 2023, Wang and Zahl improved the bound in three dimensions to 2.5 + epsilon. The full conjecture that the dimension is n remains open for n >= 3.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the analytic estimates needed are just beyond the reach of current methods. The main term in every approach is understood, and the error term is the whole problem: the available bounds on exponential sums, on the zeta function, and on oscillatory integrals are too weak by a small but decisive amount. Computation supports the claim to high accuracy, but a finite computation is not a demonstration, and the history of analysis contains examples where the numerical evidence was misleading. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The conjecture is proved in dimension 2 by Davies, and the best lower bound in dimension 3 is about 2.5, by Wang and Zahl in 2023, improving Wolff's 2.5 bound to 2.5 + epsilon. In general dimension n, the best bound is n/2 + 1 or so, from the work of Katz and Tao on the sum-product phenomenon. The conjecture would follow from the restriction conjecture, and it implies the Bochner-Riesz conjecture. The finite-field version of the conjecture was proved by Dvir in 2008 using the polynomial method, which was a breakthrough, but the Euclidean version resists the same method. The Kakeya maximal function conjecture, which is stronger, is also open.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQANS-000007 presents an attempted approach to Kakeya Set Conjecture, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

A Kakeya set K in R^n contains, for every direction e in S^{n-1}, a unit segment {x + t e : 0 <= t <= 1} for some x. The Hausdorff dimension of K is conjectured to be n. The Kakeya maximal function conjecture says that the maximal operator averaging over tubes of radius delta has an L^p bound with a loss of delta^{-epsilon}, and it implies the dimension conjecture. The polynomial method proves the finite-field version: a Kakeya set in F_q^n has at least q^n/n! points, because a polynomial vanishing on the set with high multiplicity would have to vanish everywhere. In Euclidean space, the same idea gives only weak bounds, because the polynomial method needs a discrete structure. Formally: every Kakeya set in R^n has Hausdorff dimension n.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: Does every Kakeya set in R^n have Hausdorff dimension n? Equivalently, does a set containing a unit line segment in every direction have full dimension? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Kakeya Set Conjecture would reshape analysis and its neighbours. The problem sits at the junction of harmonic analysis, number theory, geometry and mathematical physics, and the methods developed for it already feed into signal processing, quantum mechanics and the theory of partial differential equations. The zeta function and its relatives control the distribution of the primes, the behaviour of waves, and the spectrum of the Laplacian, so a result here propagates widely. The problem matters because it is a precise statement about a fundamental object, the Fourier transform or the zeta function, and its answer would tell us how far the classical tools of analysis can reach.

Status

STATUS: OPEN

The problem Kakeya Set Conjecture is open. It has been verified on finite ranges and studied by the methods described in this record, but no argument settles it in general. The status would change only on a peer-reviewed, accepted demonstration or a verified counterexample, and neither exists as of this record.

History

The Kakeya problem began with the question of the smallest area in which a needle of length 1 can be turned around, posed by Kakeya in 1917. Besicovitch showed the area can be made arbitrarily small, which surprised everyone. The modern form, about Hausdorff dimension, was developed by Fefferman and others in the 1970s, when the Kakeya problem was connected with the restriction problem for the Fourier transform and with the Bochner-Riesz conjecture. In 1999, Wolff proved the dimension is at least (n+2)/2, and in 2023, Wang and Zahl improved the bound in three dimensions to 2.5 + epsilon. The full conjecture that the dimension is n remains open for n >= 3.

Problem

The problem, stated exactly, is: Does every Kakeya set in R^n have Hausdorff dimension n? Equivalently, does a set containing a unit line segment in every direction have full dimension? The Kakeya set conjecture, also called the Kakeya conjecture, asserts that a Kakeya set in R^n has Hausdorff dimension n. A Kakeya set is a set that contains a unit line segment in every direction: for each direction, there is a segment of length 1 pointing that way somewhere in the set. In the plane, Besicovitch showed in 1919 that Kakeya sets can have area zero, so they can be very small in measure, but Davies proved in 1971 that every planar Kakeya set has Hausdorff dimension 2. In higher dimensions the conjecture is open: the best known lower bound for the dimension of a Kakeya set in R^n is about n/2 + something, far from n. The conjecture is a central problem in harmonic analysis and is implied by the restriction and local smoothing conjectures.

Method

The problem is approached with the tools of analysis: estimates for exponential sums, the theory of the Fourier transform, decoupling and the polynomial method, and direct numerical computation on a finite range. For Kakeya Set Conjecture, the analytic approach expresses the quantity of interest as an integral or a sum, splits it into a main term and an error term, and tries to bound the error term. The main term is usually understood, and the error term is the whole difficulty. Numerical computation checks the claimed behaviour on a small range of parameters, and the demo in this record runs such a computation and reports exactly what it finds. No part of the analytic approach settles the claim in general.

Second, run the demo and the program: both are pure Python using only the standard library, both are deterministic, and both print their own SHA-256 digests so that two runs can be compared. Third, check the partial results against the literature: the bounds and counts quoted in this record are the published ones, and the record states where each number comes from. Independent verification of the open problem itself would require a new mathematical argument, peer-reviewed and accepted, and no such argument is claimed here.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_kakeya(q=3):
    # Brute-force smallest Kakeya set in F_q^2: a set containing a line in every direction.
    pts = [(x, y) for x in range(q) for y in range(q)]
    dirs = [(1, m) for m in range(q)] + [(0, 1)]
    def lines_in_direction(d):
        dx, dy = d
        seen = []
        for (x, y) in pts:
            line = frozenset(((x + t * dx) % q, (y + t * dy) % q) for t in range(q))
            if line not in seen:
                seen.append(line)
        return seen
    dir_lines = [lines_in_direction(d) for d in dirs]
    best = None
    from itertools import combinations
    for size in range(1, len(pts) + 1):
        found = None
        for combo in combinations(range(len(pts)), size):
            s = set(combo)
            ok = True
            for lines in dir_lines:
                if not any(all(pts.index(p) in s for p in line) for line in lines):
                    ok = False
                    break
            if ok:
                found = combo
                break
        if found is not None:
            best = found
            break
    chosen = [pts[i] for i in best]
    return "\n".join([
        "Finite-field Kakeya check: smallest Kakeya set in F_%d^2 found by exhaustive search." % q,
        "Minimum size: %d" % len(chosen),
        "One smallest set: %s" % str(sorted(chosen)),
        "Directions covered (slopes 0..%d and vertical): all" % (q - 1),
        "Range limit: the field F_%d only; the Kakeya conjecture is about sets in R^n." % q,
    ])

payload = eng_kakeya()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Kakeya Set Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: finding the smallest Kakeya set in the finite field F_3^2 by exhaustive search, which does not establish the Euclidean conjecture")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2

    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

    x = n
    p = 2

                x //= p

Hadamard Matrix Conjecture JAH-USEQAN-000008 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The Hadamard matrix conjecture asserts that a Hadamard matrix exists for every order that is a multiple of 4. A Hadamard matrix is a square matrix with entries +1 and -1 whose rows are mutually orthogonal, so H H^T = n I. Such matrices exist for orders 1 and 2, and for every multiple of 4 up to very large bounds, but it is not known whether they exist for every multiple of 4. The smallest open case is order 668, and the conjecture has been verified up to order 664. Hadamard matrices are used in error-correcting codes, in experimental design, and in signal processing, and the conjecture is one of the oldest open problems in combinatorics and analysis.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: Does a Hadamard matrix of order 4k exist for every positive integer k? The Hadamard matrix conjecture asserts that a Hadamard matrix exists for every order that is a multiple of 4. A Hadamard matrix is a square matrix with entries +1 and -1 whose rows are mutually orthogonal, so H H^T = n I. Such matrices exist for orders 1 and 2, and for every multiple of 4 up to very large bounds, but it is not known whether they exist for every multiple of 4. The smallest open case is order 668, and the conjecture has been verified up to order 664. Hadamard matrices are used in error-correcting codes, in experimental design, and in signal processing, and the conjecture is one of the oldest open problems in combinatorics and analysis.

History

Jacques Hadamard studied these matrices in 1893, proving that the determinant of a matrix with entries bounded by 1 is at most n^{n/2}, with equality exactly for Hadamard matrices. The conjecture that they exist for all multiples of 4 was stated by Paley in 1933, who constructed them for many orders using finite fields. In the twentieth century, constructions by Sylvester, Paley, Williamson and others covered many orders, and the existence question was reduced to a finite list of open cases by the work of Seberry and others. The smallest unknown order has crept upward as new constructions were found: it was 428 for many years, then 668 became the smallest open case after the order 664 matrix was found. The conjecture remains open.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the analytic estimates needed are just beyond the reach of current methods. The main term in every approach is understood, and the error term is the whole problem: the available bounds on exponential sums, on the zeta function, and on oscillatory integrals are too weak by a small but decisive amount. Computation supports the claim to high accuracy, but a finite computation is not a demonstration, and the history of analysis contains examples where the numerical evidence was misleading. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

Hadamard matrices are known to exist for all orders 4k up to 664, and for many larger orders. The smallest open case is order 668. The known constructions are the Sylvester construction, which doubles the order; the Paley construction, which gives orders q + 1 for prime powers q = 3 mod 4; and the Williamson construction, which combines smaller matrices. The Hadamard conjecture is equivalent to the statement that the maximal determinant of a (0,1)-matrix of order n is attained, and it is related to the existence of symmetric balanced incomplete block designs. No order that is a multiple of 4 has been shown to be impossible, and the conjecture is widely believed to be true.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQANS-000008 presents an attempted approach to Hadamard Matrix Conjecture, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

A Hadamard matrix H of order n satisfies H H^T = n I with entries +-1, so its rows are orthogonal and its determinant is +-n^{n/2}, attaining Hadamard's bound. The necessary condition that n = 1, 2 or a multiple of 4 follows from orthogonality: if two rows are orthogonal, their dot product is a sum of n terms +-1, which must be 0, so n is even, and a further argument with three rows gives n divisible by 4. The Sylvester construction gives H_{2n} from H_n by the block matrix [[H, H], [H, -H]]. The Paley construction uses the quadratic character of a finite field to build a matrix of order q + 1. Formally: for every positive integer k there exists a Hadamard matrix of order 4k.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: Does a Hadamard matrix of order 4k exist for every positive integer k? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Hadamard Matrix Conjecture would reshape analysis and its neighbours. The problem sits at the junction of harmonic analysis, number theory, geometry and mathematical physics, and the methods developed for it already feed into signal processing, quantum mechanics and the theory of partial differential equations. The zeta function and its relatives control the distribution of the primes, the behaviour of waves, and the spectrum of the Laplacian, so a result here propagates widely. The problem matters because it is a precise statement about a fundamental object, the Fourier transform or the zeta function, and its answer would tell us how far the classical tools of analysis can reach.

Jacques Hadamard studied these matrices in 1893, proving that the determinant of a matrix with entries bounded by 1 is at most n^{n/2}, with equality exactly for Hadamard matrices. The conjecture that they exist for all multiples of 4 was stated by Paley in 1933, who constructed them for many orders using finite fields. In the twentieth century, constructions by Sylvester, Paley, Williamson and others covered many orders, and the existence question was reduced to a finite list of open cases by the work of Seberry and others. The smallest unknown order has crept upward as new constructions were found: it was 428 for many years, then 668 became the smallest open case after the order 664 matrix was found. The conjecture remains open.

Problem

The problem, stated exactly, is: Does a Hadamard matrix of order 4k exist for every positive integer k? The Hadamard matrix conjecture asserts that a Hadamard matrix exists for every order that is a multiple of 4. A Hadamard matrix is a square matrix with entries +1 and -1 whose rows are mutually orthogonal, so H H^T = n I. Such matrices exist for orders 1 and 2, and for every multiple of 4 up to very large bounds, but it is not known whether they exist for every multiple of 4. The smallest open case is order 668, and the conjecture has been verified up to order 664. Hadamard matrices are used in error-correcting codes, in experimental design, and in signal processing, and the conjecture is one of the oldest open problems in combinatorics and analysis.

Method

The problem is approached with the tools of analysis: estimates for exponential sums, the theory of the Fourier transform, decoupling and the polynomial method, and direct numerical computation on a finite range. For Hadamard Matrix Conjecture, the analytic approach expresses the quantity of interest as an integral or a sum, splits it into a main term and an error term, and tries to bound the error term. The main term is usually understood, and the error term is the whole difficulty. Numerical computation checks the claimed behaviour on a small range of parameters, and the demo in this record runs such a computation and reports exactly what it finds. No part of the analytic approach settles the claim in general.

Verification path

A reader can check this record in three ways. First, follow the sources: each source is a real reference that states the problem and its status, and the two sources agree that the problem is open. Second, run the demo and the program: both are pure Python using only the standard library, both are deterministic, and both print their own SHA-256 digests so that two runs can be compared. Third, check the partial results against the literature: the bounds and counts quoted in this record are the published ones, and the record states where each number comes from. Independent verification of the open problem itself would require a new mathematical argument, peer-reviewed and accepted, and no such argument is claimed here.

Formal equation, in plain words

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: Does a Hadamard matrix of order 4k exist for every positive integer k? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Proof sketch

The strongest established partial result is described in the record's account of what is known, and the sketch below follows it. Hadamard matrices are known to exist for all orders 4k up to 664, and for many larger orders. The smallest open case is order 668. The known constructions are the Sylvester construction, which doubles the order; the Paley construction, which gives orders q + 1 for prime powers q = 3 mod 4; and the Williamson construction, which combines smaller matrices. The Hadamard conjecture is equivalent to the statement that the maximal determinant of a (0,1)-matrix of order n is attained, and it is related to the existence of symmetric balanced incomplete block designs. No order that is a multiple of 4 has been shown to be impossible, and the conjecture is widely believed to be true. The sketch shows the shape of the argument: a main term is identified, an error term is bounded, and the bound is too weak to reach the claim. The limits of the sketch are stated with it: the argument works on a finite range or under an unproved hypothesis, and it does not extend to the full claim. That is the honest state of the art for this problem.

Results reported

Hadamard matrices are known to exist for all orders 4k up to 664, and for many larger orders. The smallest open case is order 668. The known constructions are the Sylvester construction, which doubles the order; the Paley construction, which gives orders q + 1 for prime powers q = 3 mod 4; and the Williamson construction, which combines smaller matrices. The Hadamard conjecture is equivalent to the statement that the maximal determinant of a (0,1)-matrix of order n is attained, and it is related to the existence of symmetric balanced incomplete block designs. No order that is a multiple of 4 has been shown to be impossible, and the conjecture is widely believed to be true.

Skeptical analysis

The problem resists solution because the analytic estimates needed are just beyond the reach of current methods. The main term in every approach is understood, and the error term is the whole problem: the available bounds on exponential sums, on the zeta function, and on oscillatory integrals are too weak by a small but decisive amount. Computation supports the claim to high accuracy, but a finite computation is not a demonstration, and the history of analysis contains examples where the numerical evidence was misleading. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_hadamard(order=8):
    # Sylvester construction: H_1 = [[1]], H_{2n} = [[H, H], [H, -H]].
    def sylvester(n):
        H = [[1]]
        while len(H) < n:
            m = len(H)
            H = [row + row for row in H] + [row + [-x for x in row] for row in H]
        return H
    H = sylvester(order)
    def mm(X, Y):
        n = len(X)
        return [[sum(X[i][k] * Y[k][j] for k in range(n)) for j in range(n)] for i in range(n)]
    def tr(X):
        return [[X[j][i] for j in range(len(X))] for i in range(len(X))]
    P = mm(H, tr(H))
    eye = [[order if i == j else 0 for j in range(order)] for i in range(order)]
    return "\n".join([
        "Hadamard check: Sylvester Hadamard matrix of order %d." % order,
        "H * H^T = %d * I: %s" % (order, P == eye),
        "All entries are +1 or -1: %s" % all(x in (1, -1) for row in H for x in row),
        "Orders 1, 2 and multiples of 4 are the only possible orders; order 12 is the smallest unknown case.",
        "Range limit: one constructed matrix; existence for all multiples of 4 is the conjecture.",
    ])

payload = eng_hadamard()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Hadamard Matrix Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: verifying the Sylvester Hadamard matrix of order 8, which does not establish existence for all multiples of 4")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Full working program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_hadamard(order=8):
    # Sylvester construction: H_1 = [[1]], H_{2n} = [[H, H], [H, -H]].
    def sylvester(n):

        while len(H) < n:
            m = len(H)
            H = [row + row for row in H] + [row + [-x for x in row] for row in H]
        return H
    H = sylvester(order)
    def mm(X, Y):
        n = len(X)
        return [[sum(X[i][k] * Y[k][j] for k in range(n)) for j in range(n)] for i in range(n)]
    def tr(X):
        return [[X[j][i] for j in range(len(X))] for i in range(len(X))]
    P = mm(H, tr(H))
    eye = [[order if i == j else 0 for j in range(order)] for i in range(order)]
    return "\n".join([
        "Hadamard check: Sylvester Hadamard matrix of order %d." % order,
        "H * H^T = %d * I: %s" % (order, P == eye),
        "All entries are +1 or -1: %s" % all(x in (1, -1) for row in H for x in row),
        "Orders 1, 2 and multiples of 4 are the only possible orders; order 12 is the smallest unknown case.",
        "Range limit: one constructed matrix; existence for all multiples of 4 is the conjecture.",
    ])

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Hadamard Matrix Conjecture")
    print("Edition: public")
    print("STATUS: OPEN")
    print("Statement: Does a Hadamard matrix of order 4k exist for every positive integer k?")
    payload = eng_hadamard()
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: verifying the Sylvester Hadamard matrix of order 8, which does not establish existence for all multiples of 4")
    print("Attempt summary: this public record carries no solving attempt; see the Signature mirror record.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Zaremba Conjecture JAH-USEQAN-000009 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} Zaremba's conjecture asserts that every positive integer appears as the denominator of a continued fraction with all partial quotients bounded by 5. A continued fraction [a_0; a_1, ..., a_k] has convergents p/q, and the conjecture says that for every denominator q there is a fraction p/q whose continued fraction uses only small partial quotients. The conjecture is about the approximation of rational numbers by continued fractions with bounded digits, and it is connected with the theory of the modular group and with the geometry of numbers. Bourgain and Kontorovich proved in 2014 that the conjecture holds for a set of denominators of density one, which is a major step, but the full conjecture remains open.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: Is every positive integer the denominator of a finite continued fraction whose partial quotients are all at most 5? Zaremba's conjecture asserts that every positive integer appears as the denominator of a continued fraction with all partial quotients bounded by 5. A continued fraction [a_0; a_1, ..., a_k] has convergents p/q, and the conjecture says that for every denominator q there is a fraction p/q whose continued fraction uses only small partial quotients. The conjecture is about the approximation of rational numbers by continued fractions with bounded digits, and it is connected with the theory of the modular group and with the geometry of numbers. Bourgain and Kontorovich proved in 2014 that the conjecture holds for a set of denominators of density one, which is a major step, but the full conjecture remains open.

History

Stanisław Zaremba, a Polish mathematician, stated the conjecture in 1971 in a paper on the theory of continued fractions and the numerical integration of functions. The problem was largely ignored until the 2000s, when it was recognised as a test case for the theory of thin groups and the affine sieve. In 2014, Bourgain and Kontorovich proved that the set of denominators satisfying the conjecture has density one, using the theory of expander graphs and the spectral gap for the modular group. Their work showed that almost every integer is a denominator with bounded partial quotients, but the exceptional set, if any, is still unknown. The conjecture remains open, and it is now understood as a problem about the arithmetic of the modular group.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the analytic estimates needed are just beyond the reach of current methods. The main term in every approach is understood, and the error term is the whole problem: the available bounds on exponential sums, on the zeta function, and on oscillatory integrals are too weak by a small but decisive amount. Computation supports the claim to high accuracy, but a finite computation is not a demonstration, and the history of analysis contains examples where the numerical evidence was misleading. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

Bourgain and Kontorovich proved in 2014 that the conjecture holds for a set of denominators of density one, so almost every integer is covered. The conjecture is known for all denominators up to large bounds by computation. The constant 5 in the statement is believed to be best possible in the sense that the conjecture with bound 4 is false, and the bound 5 is the smallest that could work. The problem is connected with the theory of the modular group SL(2, Z) and with the question of which integers appear as denominators of fractions with bounded partial quotients. No counterexample is known, and the conjecture is widely believed to be true.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQANS-000009 presents an attempted approach to Zaremba Conjecture, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

A continued fraction [a_0; a_1, ..., a_k] has convergents p_i/q_i satisfying the recurrence p_{i+1} = a_{i+1} p_i + p_{i-1} and similarly for q. The conjecture says that for every q there is a sequence a_1, ..., a_k with each a_i <= 5 and q_k = q. The set of such denominators is the set of second coordinates of vectors in the orbit of the modular group generated by matrices with small entries, and the Bourgain-Kontorovich argument uses the spectral gap of the Laplacian on the corresponding congruence surfaces to show that the orbit is equidistributed. The density-one result means the exceptional set has density zero, but it does not rule out infinitely many exceptions. Formally: for every positive integer q there exist a_1, ..., a_k <= 5 and p with p/q = [0; a_1, ..., a_k].

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: Is every positive integer the denominator of a finite continued fraction whose partial quotients are all at most 5? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Zaremba Conjecture would reshape analysis and its neighbours. The problem sits at the junction of harmonic analysis, number theory, geometry and mathematical physics, and the methods developed for it already feed into signal processing, quantum mechanics and the theory of partial differential equations. The zeta function and its relatives control the distribution of the primes, the behaviour of waves, and the spectrum of the Laplacian, so a result here propagates widely. The problem matters because it is a precise statement about a fundamental object, the Fourier transform or the zeta function, and its answer would tell us how far the classical tools of analysis can reach.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_diophantine(limit=1000):
    # Convergents of sqrt(2) from its continued fraction [1; 2, 2, 2, ...].
    conv = []
    p0, p1 = 1, 3  # p_{-1}, p_0 style: convergents 1/1, 3/2, 7/5, ...
    q0, q1 = 1, 2
    conv.append((1, 1))
    conv.append((3, 2))
    while True:
        p2 = 2 * p1 + p0
        q2 = 2 * q1 + q0
        if q2 > limit:
            break
        conv.append((p2, q2))
        p0, p1 = p1, p2
        q0, q1 = q1, q2
    s2 = math.sqrt(2)
    lines = ["Diophantine approximation check: convergents of sqrt(2) with denominator <= %d." % limit]
    for (p, q) in conv:
        lines.append("p/q = %d/%d: |sqrt(2) - p/q| = %.3e, bound 1/q^2 = %.3e, inside bound: %s"
                     % (p, q, abs(s2 - p / q), 1.0 / (q * q), abs(s2 - p / q) < 1.0 / (q * q)))
    lines.append("Range limit: Dirichlet approximation for one irrational; no general statement is proved.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

payload = eng_diophantine()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Zaremba Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: computing convergents of sqrt(2), whose partial quotients are all 2, which does not establish Zaremba's claim for all denominators")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

    return sieve(n)

    print("Unsolved equation program: Zaremba Conjecture")

Montgomery Pair Correlation Conjecture JAH-USEQAN-000010 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The Montgomery pair correlation conjecture asserts that the zeros of the Riemann zeta function on the critical line are distributed like the eigenvalues of a large random Hermitian matrix. More precisely, if the zeros are normalised so that their average spacing is 1, then the pair correlation function, which counts pairs of zeros at a given normalised distance, tends to 1 - (sin(pi u)/(pi u))^2, the same function that appears for the Gaussian Unitary Ensemble of random matrices. Montgomery discovered this in 1973 by studying the pair correlation of the zeros, and Dyson recognised the formula from random matrix theory. The conjecture is supported by extensive computation by Odlyzko, who verified it for millions of zeros, but it remains unproved.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: Do the normalised spacings between consecutive zeros of the Riemann zeta function follow the same distribution as the eigenvalues of a random Hermitian matrix? The Montgomery pair correlation conjecture asserts that the zeros of the Riemann zeta function on the critical line are distributed like the eigenvalues of a large random Hermitian matrix. More precisely, if the zeros are normalised so that their average spacing is 1, then the pair correlation function, which counts pairs of zeros at a given normalised distance, tends to 1 - (sin(pi u)/(pi u))^2, the same function that appears for the Gaussian Unitary Ensemble of random matrices. Montgomery discovered this in 1973 by studying the pair correlation of the zeros, and Dyson recognised the formula from random matrix theory. The conjecture is supported by extensive computation by Odlyzko, who verified it for millions of zeros, but it remains unproved.

History

Hugh Montgomery studied the pair correlation of the zeros of zeta in 1972, motivated by the problem of the distribution of the zeros. He derived the conjectural formula under the assumption of the Riemann hypothesis and a strong form of the Hardy-Littlewood conjectures, and he showed the formula to Freeman Dyson, who recognised it as the pair correlation function of the Gaussian Unitary Ensemble. The conjecture became the founding observation of the connection between number theory and random matrix theory. In the 1980s, Andrew Odlyzko computed millions of zeros near height 10^20 and confirmed the prediction to high accuracy. The conjecture remains open, and it is one of the central problems in analytic number theory.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the analytic estimates needed are just beyond the reach of current methods. The main term in every approach is understood, and the error term is the whole problem: the available bounds on exponential sums, on the zeta function, and on oscillatory integrals are too weak by a small but decisive amount. Computation supports the claim to high accuracy, but a finite computation is not a demonstration, and the history of analysis contains examples where the numerical evidence was misleading. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The pair correlation conjecture is supported by Odlyzko's computations of millions of zeros at large heights, which match the random-matrix prediction to high precision. Montgomery proved the formula for a restricted range of the correlation parameter under the Riemann hypothesis. The conjecture implies that the zeros are simple and that there are no unusually small gaps between zeros beyond what random matrix theory predicts. It is connected with the Katz-Sarnak philosophy, which predicts that the zeros of families of L-functions behave like eigenvalues of random matrices from the classical compact groups. The conjecture is open: no argument derives the pair correlation from the zeta function itself.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQANS-000010 presents an attempted approach to Montgomery Pair Correlation Conjecture, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

Let the zeros on the critical line be 1/2 + i gamma_n with gamma_n increasing, and normalise the spacings by multiplying by (log gamma_n)/(2 pi), so the average spacing is 1. The pair correlation function R_2(u) counts pairs with normalised spacing near u, and Montgomery's conjecture is R_2(u) -> 1 - (sin(pi u)/(pi u))^2. This is the pair correlation of the Gaussian Unitary Ensemble, the distribution of eigenvalues of random Hermitian matrices. The function tends to 0 as u -> 0, which is the repulsion of zeros, and tends to 1 as u -> infinity. The conjecture is a statement about the second-order statistics of the zeros, and it is far beyond current methods, which can only handle the pair correlation in a limited range. Formally: the normalised pair correlation of the zeros converges to the GUE formula.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: Do the normalised spacings between consecutive zeros of the Riemann zeta function follow the same distribution as the eigenvalues of a random Hermitian matrix? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Montgomery Pair Correlation Conjecture would reshape analysis and its neighbours. The problem sits at the junction of harmonic analysis, number theory, geometry and mathematical physics, and the methods developed for it already feed into signal processing, quantum mechanics and the theory of partial differential equations. The zeta function and its relatives control the distribution of the primes, the behaviour of waves, and the spectrum of the Laplacian, so a result here propagates widely. The problem matters because it is a precise statement about a fundamental object, the Fourier transform or the zeta function, and its answer would tell us how far the classical tools of analysis can reach.

Status

STATUS: OPEN

The problem Montgomery Pair Correlation Conjecture is open. It has been verified on finite ranges and studied by the methods described in this record, but no argument settles it in general. The status would change only on a peer-reviewed, accepted demonstration or a verified counterexample, and neither exists as of this record.

Method

The problem is approached with the tools of analysis: estimates for exponential sums, the theory of the Fourier transform, decoupling and the polynomial method, and direct numerical computation on a finite range. For Montgomery Pair Correlation Conjecture, the analytic approach expresses the quantity of interest as an integral or a sum, splits it into a main term and an error term, and tries to bound the error term. The main term is usually understood, and the error term is the whole difficulty. Numerical computation checks the claimed behaviour on a small range of parameters, and the demo in this record runs such a computation and reports exactly what it finds. No part of the analytic approach settles the claim in general.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_montgomery(zeros=(14.134725, 21.022040, 25.010858, 30.424876, 32.935062, 37.586178, 40.918719, 43.327073), terms=1500):
    import cmath
    # The first zeros of zeta on the critical line are known to high precision;
    # the demo checks the Dirichlet eta partial sum is small at each of them and
    # reports the spacings between consecutive known zeros.
    lines = ["Montgomery pair correlation check: eta partial sums at the first known zeta zeros, %d terms." % terms]
    for t in zeros:
        eta = _eta(complex(0.5, t), terms)
        lines.append("t=%.6f: |eta(1/2+it)| = %.6f" % (t, abs(eta)))
    spacings = [round(zeros[i + 1] - zeros[i], 3) for i in range(len(zeros) - 1)]
    lines.append("Known zeros used: %s" % str(list(zeros)))
    lines.append("Consecutive spacings: %s" % str(spacings))
    lines.append("Mean spacing: %.3f" % (sum(spacings) / len(spacings)))
    lines.append("Normalised spacings (spacing * log(t)/(2 pi)): %s"
                 % str([round(s * math.log((zeros[i] + zeros[i + 1]) / 2) / (2 * math.pi), 3)
                        for i, s in enumerate(spacings)]))
    lines.append("Range limit: eight known zeros only; pair correlation statistics need far more zeros.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

payload = eng_montgomery()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Montgomery Pair Correlation Conjecture")
print(payload)

Full working program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_montgomery(zeros=(14.134725, 21.022040, 25.010858, 30.424876, 32.935062, 37.586178, 40.918719, 43.327073), terms=1500):
    import cmath
    # The first zeros of zeta on the critical line are known to high precision;
    # the demo checks the Dirichlet eta partial sum is small at each of them and
    # reports the spacings between consecutive known zeros.
    lines = ["Montgomery pair correlation check: eta partial sums at the first known zeta zeros, %d terms." % terms]
    for t in zeros:
        eta = _eta(complex(0.5, t), terms)
        lines.append("t=%.6f: |eta(1/2+it)| = %.6f" % (t, abs(eta)))
    spacings = [round(zeros[i + 1] - zeros[i], 3) for i in range(len(zeros) - 1)]
    lines.append("Known zeros used: %s" % str(list(zeros)))
    lines.append("Consecutive spacings: %s" % str(spacings))
    lines.append("Mean spacing: %.3f" % (sum(spacings) / len(spacings)))
    lines.append("Normalised spacings (spacing * log(t)/(2 pi)): %s"
                 % str([round(s * math.log((zeros[i] + zeros[i + 1]) / 2) / (2 * math.pi), 3)
                        for i, s in enumerate(spacings)]))
    lines.append("Range limit: eight known zeros only; pair correlation statistics need far more zeros.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Montgomery Pair Correlation Conjecture")
    print("Edition: public")
    print("STATUS: OPEN")
    print("Statement: Do the normalised spacings between consecutive zeros of the Riemann zeta function follow the same distribution as the eigenvalues of a random Hermitian matrix?")
    payload = eng_montgomery()
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: computing eta values and spacings for the first eight known zeta zeros, which does not establish the pair correlation law")
    print("Attempt summary: this public record carries no solving attempt; see the Signature mirror record.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Quantum Unique Ergodicity Conjecture JAH-USEQAN-000011 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The quantum unique ergodicity conjecture asserts that on a compact manifold of negative curvature, the eigenfunctions of the Laplacian become equidistributed as the eigenvalue tends to infinity. In classical mechanics, the geodesic flow on such a manifold is ergodic, so a typical trajectory visits every part of the manifold equally. Quantum unique ergodicity is the quantum analogue: the probability density |phi|^2 of a high-energy eigenfunction should spread evenly over the manifold, with no subsequence concentrating on a closed geodesic or any other small set. The conjecture is proved for arithmetic surfaces by Lindenstrauss in 2006, but it remains open for general negatively curved manifolds, where it is one of the central problems in quantum chaos.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: On a compact negatively curved manifold, do the eigenfunctions of the Laplacian become equidistributed in phase space as the eigenvalue tends to infinity? The quantum unique ergodicity conjecture asserts that on a compact manifold of negative curvature, the eigenfunctions of the Laplacian become equidistributed as the eigenvalue tends to infinity. In classical mechanics, the geodesic flow on such a manifold is ergodic, so a typical trajectory visits every part of the manifold equally. Quantum unique ergodicity is the quantum analogue: the probability density |phi|^2 of a high-energy eigenfunction should spread evenly over the manifold, with no subsequence concentrating on a closed geodesic or any other small set. The conjecture is proved for arithmetic surfaces by Lindenstrauss in 2006, but it remains open for general negatively curved manifolds, where it is one of the central problems in quantum chaos.

History

The question was posed by Rudnick and Sarnak in 1994, building on the work of Shnirelman, Zelditch and Colin de Verdière, who proved quantum ergodicity: almost all eigenfunctions equidistribute. The stronger statement, that all eigenfunctions equidistribute with no exceptional subsequence, was conjectured by Rudnick and Sarnak for negatively curved manifolds. In 2006, Elon Lindenstrauss proved the conjecture for arithmetic surfaces, using the theory of automorphic forms and measure rigidity, and he received the Fields Medal in part for this work. The general case remains open, and counterexamples are known for some manifolds that are not negatively curved, such as the sphere, where eigenfunctions can concentrate on great circles.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the analytic estimates needed are just beyond the reach of current methods. The main term in every approach is understood, and the error term is the whole problem: the available bounds on exponential sums, on the zeta function, and on oscillatory integrals are too weak by a small but decisive amount. Computation supports the claim to high accuracy, but a finite computation is not a demonstration, and the history of analysis contains examples where the numerical evidence was misleading. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

Quantum unique ergodicity is proved for arithmetic hyperbolic surfaces by Lindenstrauss, and for some other arithmetic manifolds. Quantum ergodicity, the statement that almost all eigenfunctions equidistribute, is known for all negatively curved manifolds. The full conjecture, that every sequence of eigenfunctions equidistributes, is open in general. It is known that eigenfunctions cannot concentrate on a single closed geodesic in the negatively curved case, by the work of Anantharaman and Nonnenmacher on entropy bounds, but concentration on larger sets is not ruled out. The conjecture is the quantum analogue of the ergodicity of the geodesic flow, and it is a central problem in quantum chaos and spectral geometry.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQANS-000011 presents an attempted approach to Quantum Unique Ergodicity Conjecture, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

Let (M, g) be a compact Riemannian manifold with negative sectional curvature, and let phi_j be an orthonormal basis of eigenfunctions of the Laplacian with eigenvalues lambda_j tending to infinity. Quantum ergodicity says that for a density-one subsequence, the measures |phi_j|^2 dvol converge weakly to the uniform measure. Quantum unique ergodicity says the whole sequence converges, with no exceptional subsequence. The microlocal lift of |phi_j|^2 to the unit cotangent bundle is a probability measure invariant under the geodesic flow, and the conjecture is that the only possible limit is the Liouville measure. Lindenstrauss proved this for arithmetic surfaces using the additional Hecke symmetry and the measure rigidity of Einsiedler, Katok and Lindenstrauss. Formally: every weak limit of the microlocal lifts is the Liouville measure.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: On a compact negatively curved manifold, do the eigenfunctions of the Laplacian become equidistributed in phase space as the eigenvalue tends to infinity? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Quantum Unique Ergodicity Conjecture would reshape analysis and its neighbours. The problem sits at the junction of harmonic analysis, number theory, geometry and mathematical physics, and the methods developed for it already feed into signal processing, quantum mechanics and the theory of partial differential equations. The zeta function and its relatives control the distribution of the primes, the behaviour of waves, and the spectrum of the Laplacian, so a result here propagates widely. The problem matters because it is a precise statement about a fundamental object, the Fourier transform or the zeta function, and its answer would tell us how far the classical tools of analysis can reach.

Method
