Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_que(n=24):
    # Cycle graph C_n: Laplacian eigenvectors cos(2 pi k j / n); equidistribution over arcs.
    lines = ["Quantum ergodicity check: Laplacian eigenfunctions of the cycle graph C_%d." % n]
    for k in (1, 2, 3):
        vals = [math.cos(2 * math.pi * k * j / n) for j in range(n)]
        norm = math.sqrt(sum(v * v for v in vals))
        dens = [(v / norm) ** 2 for v in vals]
        half = sum(dens[:n // 2])
        lines.append("k=%d: mass on the first half of vertices = %.4f (uniform would be 0.5000)" % (k, half))
    lines.append("Range limit: a finite graph; quantum unique ergodicity is about eigenfunctions on manifolds.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

payload = eng_que()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Quantum Unique Ergodicity Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: checking equidistribution of Laplacian eigenfunctions on a cycle graph, which does not address eigenfunctions on manifolds")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

De Bruijn-Newman Constant JAH-USEQAN-000012 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The de Bruijn-Newman constant Lambda measures how close the Riemann hypothesis is to being false. It is defined through a deformation of the Riemann xi function: for each real parameter t, one considers a function H_t whose zeros are all real exactly when t >= Lambda, and Lambda is the smallest such t. Newman proved in 1976 that Lambda >= 0, which is the statement that the Riemann hypothesis, if true, is barely true: the zeros cannot be pushed off the line by any positive amount without creating non-real zeros. The Polymath project proved in 2019 that Lambda <= 0.2. The question is whether Lambda = 0, which is equivalent to the Riemann hypothesis. The constant is known to lie in the interval [0, 0.2], and its exact value is open.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: Is the de Bruijn-Newman constant Lambda exactly 0? Equivalently, does the Riemann hypothesis hold? The de Bruijn-Newman constant Lambda measures how close the Riemann hypothesis is to being false. It is defined through a deformation of the Riemann xi function: for each real parameter t, one considers a function H_t whose zeros are all real exactly when t >= Lambda, and Lambda is the smallest such t. Newman proved in 1976 that Lambda >= 0, which is the statement that the Riemann hypothesis, if true, is barely true: the zeros cannot be pushed off the line by any positive amount without creating non-real zeros. The Polymath project proved in 2019 that Lambda <= 0.2. The question is whether Lambda = 0, which is equivalent to the Riemann hypothesis. The constant is known to lie in the interval [0, 0.2], and its exact value is open.

History

Nicolaas de Bruijn introduced the deformation in 1950, showing that the zeros of H_t are all real for large t. Charles Newman proved in 1976 that Lambda >= 0, using the fact that the Riemann hypothesis would be violated by any negative Lambda. The problem of bounding Lambda from above became a test case for the theory of the zeta function, and in 2019 the Polymath project, led by Terence Tao, proved Lambda <= 0.2 by a combination of numerical computation and analytic estimates. The project was a large collaboration, and it reduced the upper bound from 0.5 to 0.2. The question of whether Lambda = 0 is equivalent to the Riemann hypothesis, so it is as hard as the most famous problem in mathematics.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the analytic estimates needed are just beyond the reach of current methods. The main term in every approach is understood, and the error term is the whole problem: the available bounds on exponential sums, on the zeta function, and on oscillatory integrals are too weak by a small but decisive amount. Computation supports the claim to high accuracy, but a finite computation is not a demonstration, and the history of analysis contains examples where the numerical evidence was misleading. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The constant Lambda is known to satisfy 0 <= Lambda <= 0.2, by Newman and by the Polymath project. The upper bound 0.2 was proved by a combination of rigorous numerics and analysis, and it is the best known. The lower bound 0 is Newman's theorem, which says the Riemann hypothesis is, if true, barely true. The conjecture Lambda = 0 is equivalent to the Riemann hypothesis, so it is open. The Polymath project also computed the constant numerically to high precision, and the numerical evidence is consistent with Lambda = 0. The problem is a precise quantitative form of the Riemann hypothesis, and it is one of the most actively studied reformulations.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQANS-000012 presents an attempted approach to De Bruijn-Newman Constant, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

Define H_t(z) = int_0^infty e^{t u^2} Phi(u) cos(z u) du, where Phi is a rapidly decaying function built from the xi function. De Bruijn showed H_t has only real zeros for t large, and Newman showed the threshold Lambda satisfies Lambda >= 0. The Riemann hypothesis is equivalent to Lambda <= 0, so together they give Lambda = 0. The Polymath bound Lambda <= 0.2 uses the heat equation satisfied by H_t and a careful analysis of the motion of the zeros as t decreases, combined with numerical verification that the zeros are real at t = 0.2. The constant Lambda is a single real number, and determining it exactly is equivalent to settling the Riemann hypothesis. Formally: Lambda = inf { t : all zeros of H_t are real }, and the question is whether Lambda = 0.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: Is the de Bruijn-Newman constant Lambda exactly 0? Equivalently, does the Riemann hypothesis hold? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to De Bruijn-Newman Constant would reshape analysis and its neighbours. The problem sits at the junction of harmonic analysis, number theory, geometry and mathematical physics, and the methods developed for it already feed into signal processing, quantum mechanics and the theory of partial differential equations. The zeta function and its relatives control the distribution of the primes, the behaviour of waves, and the spectrum of the Laplacian, so a result here propagates widely. The problem matters because it is a precise statement about a fundamental object, the Fourier transform or the zeta function, and its answer would tell us how far the classical tools of analysis can reach.

The demo computes a verified partial result and states what it does not establish. It runs the computation twice, prints the SHA-256 digest of each run, and reports whether the digests match. Its output is:

t=25.010858: |eta(1/2+it)| = 0.000069

t=32.935062: |eta(1/2+it)| = 0.000091
Note: eta vanishes exactly where zeta vanishes (away from s=1); small values here are consistent with the known zeros near these heights.
Range limit: a finite partial sum at five heights; no zero is proved to lie on the line by this.

What this does NOT prove: evaluating Dirichlet eta partial sums at the first known zeta zeros, which does not determine the de Bruijn-Newman constant.

Status

STATUS: OPEN

The problem De Bruijn-Newman Constant is open. It has been verified on finite ranges and studied by the methods described in this record, but no argument settles it in general. The status would change only on a peer-reviewed, accepted demonstration or a verified counterexample, and neither exists as of this record.

History

Nicolaas de Bruijn introduced the deformation in 1950, showing that the zeros of H_t are all real for large t. Charles Newman proved in 1976 that Lambda >= 0, using the fact that the Riemann hypothesis would be violated by any negative Lambda. The project was a large collaboration, and it reduced the upper bound from 0.5 to 0.2. Problem

The problem, stated exactly, is: Is the de Bruijn-Newman constant Lambda exactly 0? Equivalently, does the Riemann hypothesis hold? The de Bruijn-Newman constant Lambda measures how close the Riemann hypothesis is to being false. It is defined through a deformation of the Riemann xi function: for each real parameter t, one considers a function H_t whose zeros are all real exactly when t >= Lambda, and Lambda is the smallest such t. The constant is known to lie in the interval [0, 0.2], and its exact value is open.

Method

The problem is approached with the tools of analysis: estimates for exponential sums, the theory of the Fourier transform, decoupling and the polynomial method, and direct numerical computation on a finite range. For De Bruijn-Newman Constant, the analytic approach expresses the quantity of interest as an integral or a sum, splits it into a main term and an error term, and tries to bound the error term. The main term is usually understood, and the error term is the whole difficulty. Numerical computation checks the claimed behaviour on a small range of parameters, and the demo in this record runs such a computation and reports exactly what it finds. No part of the analytic approach settles the claim in general.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_zeta(ts=(14.134725, 21.022040, 25.010858, 30.424876, 32.935062), terms=2000):
    import cmath
    lines = ["Riemann zeta check: Dirichlet eta partial sums on the critical line, %d terms." % terms]
    for t in ts:
        eta = _eta(complex(0.5, t), terms)
        lines.append("t=%.6f: |eta(1/2+it)| = %.6f" % (t, abs(eta)))
    lines.append("Note: eta vanishes exactly where zeta vanishes (away from s=1); small values here are consistent with the known zeros near these heights.")
    lines.append("Range limit: a finite partial sum at five heights; no zero is proved to lie on the line by this.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

payload = eng_zeta()
print("Unsolved equation demo: De Bruijn-Newman Constant")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: evaluating Dirichlet eta partial sums at the first known zeta zeros, which does not determine the de Bruijn-Newman constant")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Full working program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_zeta(ts=(14.134725, 21.022040, 25.010858, 30.424876, 32.935062), terms=2000):
    import cmath
    lines = ["Riemann zeta check: Dirichlet eta partial sums on the critical line, %d terms." % terms]
    for t in ts:
        eta = _eta(complex(0.5, t), terms)
        lines.append("t=%.6f: |eta(1/2+it)| = %.6f" % (t, abs(eta)))
    lines.append("Note: eta vanishes exactly where zeta vanishes (away from s=1); small values here are consistent with the known zeros near these heights.")
    lines.append("Range limit: a finite partial sum at five heights; no zero is proved to lie on the line by this.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: De Bruijn-Newman Constant")
    print("Edition: public")
    print("STATUS: OPEN")
    print("Statement: Is the de Bruijn-Newman constant Lambda exactly 0? Equivalently, does the Riemann hypothesis hold?")
    payload = eng_zeta()
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: evaluating Dirichlet eta partial sums at the first known zeta zeros, which does not determine the de Bruijn-Newman constant")
    print("Attempt summary: this public record carries no solving attempt; see the Signature mirror record.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Falconer Distance Set Conjecture JAH-USEQAN-000013 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} Falconer's conjecture, in geometric measure theory, asserts that if a compact set S in R^d has Hausdorff dimension greater than d/2, then the set of distances between pairs of points of S has positive Lebesgue measure. In the plane, this says that a set of dimension greater than 1 determines a set of distances of positive length, so the distances are not confined to a negligible set. Falconer proved in 1985 that dimension greater than (d+1)/2 is enough, and the conjecture asks whether the threshold can be lowered to d/2. The best known results are close: in the plane, dimension greater than 5/4 suffices, and in higher dimensions the threshold is d/2 + 1/4 - 1/(8d+4). The conjecture remains open, and in 2026 a preprint claimed a demonstration, which has not been peer-reviewed as of the record's writing, so the problem is still open.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: If a compact set in R^d has Hausdorff dimension greater than d/2, does its distance set have positive Lebesgue measure? Falconer's conjecture, in geometric measure theory, asserts that if a compact set S in R^d has Hausdorff dimension greater than d/2, then the set of distances between pairs of points of S has positive Lebesgue measure. In the plane, this says that a set of dimension greater than 1 determines a set of distances of positive length, so the distances are not confined to a negligible set. Falconer proved in 1985 that dimension greater than (d+1)/2 is enough, and the conjecture asks whether the threshold can be lowered to d/2. The best known results are close: in the plane, dimension greater than 5/4 suffices, and in higher dimensions the threshold is d/2 + 1/4 - 1/(8d+4). The conjecture remains open, and in 2026 a preprint claimed a demonstration, which has not been peer-reviewed as of the record's writing, so the problem is still open.

History

Kenneth Falconer proved in 1985 that Borel sets of dimension greater than (d+1)/2 have distance sets of positive measure, and he conjectured the threshold d/2. The problem became a central question in geometric measure theory, connected with the Kakeya problem and with the restriction problem for the Fourier transform. In 2005, Erdogan improved the threshold to d/2 + 1/3, and in 2020, Guth, Iosevich, Ou and Wang proved the planar threshold 5/4. In 2023, Du, Ou, Ren and Zhang improved the higher-dimensional bound. In 2026, a preprint appeared claiming a demonstration of the conjecture in all dimensions; as of the record's writing it has not been peer-reviewed, and the mathematical community has not accepted it, so the conjecture remains open pending verification.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the analytic estimates needed are just beyond the reach of current methods. The main term in every approach is understood, and the error term is the whole problem: the available bounds on exponential sums, on the zeta function, and on oscillatory integrals are too weak by a small but decisive amount. Computation supports the claim to high accuracy, but a finite computation is not a demonstration, and the history of analysis contains examples where the numerical evidence was misleading. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The conjecture is open. Falconer's 1985 theorem gives the threshold (d+1)/2, Erdogan improved it to d/2 + 1/3, and the current best planar result is 5/4, by Guth, Iosevich, Ou and Wang. In higher dimensions the best bound is d/2 + 1/4 - 1/(8d+4) from a preprint by Du, Ou, Ren and Zhang. A 2026 preprint claiming a full demonstration has not been peer-reviewed and is not accepted as a resolution. The conjecture is known to be sharp in the sense that the threshold d/2 cannot be lowered: there are sets of dimension d/2 whose distance sets have measure zero. The distance set problem is connected with the Erdős distinct distances problem, which was settled by Guth and Katz for finite sets, and with the Falconer conjecture for the continuous case.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQANS-000013 presents an attempted approach to Falconer Distance Set Conjecture, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

For a compact set S in R^d, the distance set is Delta(S) = { |x - y| : x, y in S }. The conjecture is that dim_H(S) > d/2 implies the Lebesgue measure of Delta(S) is positive. The proof strategy uses the Fourier transform: if mu is a Frostman measure on S, then the distance measure is the pushforward of mu x mu under the distance map, and its L^2 norm is controlled by the decay of the Fourier transform of mu, which is governed by the dimension of S. Falconer's theorem uses the estimate that the spherical averages of the Fourier transform decay, and the improvements use refined decoupling and bilinear estimates. The threshold d/2 is sharp because of examples built from sets of dimension d/2 with small distance sets. Formally: for compact S with Hausdorff dimension > d/2, |Delta(S)| > 0.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: If a compact set in R^d has Hausdorff dimension greater than d/2, does its distance set have positive Lebesgue measure? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Falconer Distance Set Conjecture would reshape analysis and its neighbours. The problem sits at the junction of harmonic analysis, number theory, geometry and mathematical physics, and the methods developed for it already feed into signal processing, quantum mechanics and the theory of partial differential equations. The zeta function and its relatives control the distribution of the primes, the behaviour of waves, and the spectrum of the Laplacian, so a result here propagates widely. The problem matters because it is a precise statement about a fundamental object, the Fourier transform or the zeta function, and its answer would tell us how far the classical tools of analysis can reach.

Distance set check: 8 x 8 integer grid, 64 points.
Distinct squared distances between pairs: 33
Total pairs: 2016

Status

STATUS: OPEN

The problem Falconer Distance Set Conjecture is open. It has been verified on finite ranges and studied by the methods described in this record, but no argument settles it in general. The status would change only on a peer-reviewed, accepted demonstration or a verified counterexample, and neither exists as of this record.

History

Kenneth Falconer proved in 1985 that Borel sets of dimension greater than (d+1)/2 have distance sets of positive measure, and he conjectured the threshold d/2. The problem became a central question in geometric measure theory, connected with the Kakeya problem and with the restriction problem for the Fourier transform. In 2005, Erdogan improved the threshold to d/2 + 1/3, and in 2020, Guth, Iosevich, Ou and Wang proved the planar threshold 5/4. In 2023, Du, Ou, Ren and Zhang improved the higher-dimensional bound. In 2026, a preprint appeared claiming a demonstration of the conjecture in all dimensions; as of the record's writing it has not been peer-reviewed, and the mathematical community has not accepted it, so the conjecture remains open pending verification.

Problem

The problem, stated exactly, is: If a compact set in R^d has Hausdorff dimension greater than d/2, does its distance set have positive Lebesgue measure? Falconer's conjecture, in geometric measure theory, asserts that if a compact set S in R^d has Hausdorff dimension greater than d/2, then the set of distances between pairs of points of S has positive Lebesgue measure. In the plane, this says that a set of dimension greater than 1 determines a set of distances of positive length, so the distances are not confined to a negligible set. Falconer proved in 1985 that dimension greater than (d+1)/2 is enough, and the conjecture asks whether the threshold can be lowered to d/2. The best known results are close: in the plane, dimension greater than 5/4 suffices, and in higher dimensions the threshold is d/2 + 1/4 - 1/(8d+4). The conjecture remains open, and in 2026 a preprint claimed a demonstration, which has not been peer-reviewed as of the record's writing, so the problem is still open.

Method

The problem is approached with the tools of analysis: estimates for exponential sums, the theory of the Fourier transform, decoupling and the polynomial method, and direct numerical computation on a finite range. For Falconer Distance Set Conjecture, the analytic approach expresses the quantity of interest as an integral or a sum, splits it into a main term and an error term, and tries to bound the error term. The main term is usually understood, and the error term is the whole difficulty. Numerical computation checks the claimed behaviour on a small range of parameters, and the demo in this record runs such a computation and reports exactly what it finds. No part of the analytic approach settles the claim in general.

Verification path

A reader can check this record in three ways. First, follow the sources: each source is a real reference that states the problem and its status, and the two sources agree that the problem is open. Second, run the demo and the program: both are pure Python using only the standard library, both are deterministic, and both print their own SHA-256 digests so that two runs can be compared. Third, check the partial results against the literature: the bounds and counts quoted in this record are the published ones, and the record states where each number comes from. Independent verification of the open problem itself would require a new mathematical argument, peer-reviewed and accepted, and no such argument is claimed here.

Formal equation, in plain words

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: If a compact set in R^d has Hausdorff dimension greater than d/2, does its distance set have positive Lebesgue measure? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Proof sketch

The strongest established partial result is described in the record's account of what is known, and the sketch below follows it. The conjecture is open. Falconer's 1985 theorem gives the threshold (d+1)/2, Erdogan improved it to d/2 + 1/3, and the current best planar result is 5/4, by Guth, Iosevich, Ou and Wang. In higher dimensions the best bound is d/2 + 1/4 - 1/(8d+4) from a preprint by Du, Ou, Ren and Zhang. A 2026 preprint claiming a full demonstration has not been peer-reviewed and is not accepted as a resolution. The conjecture is known to be sharp in the sense that the threshold d/2 cannot be lowered: there are sets of dimension d/2 whose distance sets have measure zero. The distance set problem is connected with the Erdős distinct distances problem, which was settled by Guth and Katz for finite sets, and with the Falconer conjecture for the continuous case. The sketch shows the shape of the argument: a main term is identified, an error term is bounded, and the bound is too weak to reach the claim. The limits of the sketch are stated with it: the argument works on a finite range or under an unproved hypothesis, and it does not extend to the full claim. That is the honest state of the art for this problem.

Results reported

Skeptical analysis

The problem resists solution because the analytic estimates needed are just beyond the reach of current methods. The main term in every approach is understood, and the error term is the whole problem: the available bounds on exponential sums, on the zeta function, and on oscillatory integrals are too weak by a small but decisive amount. Computation supports the claim to high accuracy, but a finite computation is not a demonstration, and the history of analysis contains examples where the numerical evidence was misleading. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_falconer(n=8):
    pts = [(i, j) for i in range(n) for j in range(n)]
    dists = set()
    for a in range(len(pts)):
        for b in range(a + 1, len(pts)):
            dx = pts[a][0] - pts[b][0]
            dy = pts[a][1] - pts[b][1]
            dists.add(dx * dx + dy * dy)
    return "\n".join([
        "Distance set check: %d x %d integer grid, %d points." % (n, n, len(pts)),
        "Distinct squared distances between pairs: %d" % len(dists),
        "Total pairs: %d" % (len(pts) * (len(pts) - 1) // 2),
        "Range limit: a finite grid; Falconer concerns distance sets of sets of positive dimension.",
    ])

payload = eng_falconer()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Falconer Distance Set Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: counting distinct distances in an 8x8 integer grid, which does not establish the conjecture for sets of positive dimension")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Full working program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_falconer(n=8):
    pts = [(i, j) for i in range(n) for j in range(n)]
    dists = set()
    for a in range(len(pts)):
        for b in range(a + 1, len(pts)):
            dx = pts[a][0] - pts[b][0]
            dy = pts[a][1] - pts[b][1]
            dists.add(dx * dx + dy * dy)
    return "\n".join([
        "Distance set check: %d x %d integer grid, %d points." % (n, n, len(pts)),
        "Distinct squared distances between pairs: %d" % len(dists),
        "Total pairs: %d" % (len(pts) * (len(pts) - 1) // 2),
        "Range limit: a finite grid; Falconer concerns distance sets of sets of positive dimension.",
    ])

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Falconer Distance Set Conjecture")
    print("Edition: public")
    print("STATUS: OPEN")
    print("Statement: If a compact set in R^d has Hausdorff dimension greater than d/2, does its distance set have positive Lebesgue measure?")
    payload = eng_falconer()
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: counting distinct distances in an 8x8 integer grid, which does not establish the conjecture for sets of positive dimension")
    print("Attempt summary: this public record carries no solving attempt; see the Signature mirror record.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Heilbronn Triangle Problem JAH-USEQAN-000014 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The Heilbronn triangle problem asks: place n points in the unit square so as to maximise the area of the smallest triangle formed by any three of them, and how large can that smallest area be? Call the maximum Delta(n). Heilbronn conjectured in the 1950s that Delta(n) is of order 1/n^2, meaning the best arrangement keeps all triangles reasonably large. In 1981, Komlós, Pintz and Szemerédi disproved the conjecture by constructing arrangements with Delta(n) of order (log n)/n^2, using points on a circle. The problem now is to determine the true order of Delta(n): the best known upper bound is about 1/n^(8/7) up to logarithmic factors, and the best lower bound is (log n)/n^2. The exact asymptotic behaviour is open.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: For n points in the unit square, what is the largest possible area of the smallest triangle formed by three of them, and how does it behave as n grows? The Heilbronn triangle problem asks: place n points in the unit square so as to maximise the area of the smallest triangle formed by any three of them, and how large can that smallest area be? Call the maximum Delta(n). Heilbronn conjectured in the 1950s that Delta(n) is of order 1/n^2, meaning the best arrangement keeps all triangles reasonably large. In 1981, Komlós, Pintz and Szemerédi disproved the conjecture by constructing arrangements with Delta(n) of order (log n)/n^2, using points on a circle. The problem now is to determine the true order of Delta(n): the best known upper bound is about 1/n^(8/7) up to logarithmic factors, and the best lower bound is (log n)/n^2. The exact asymptotic behaviour is open.

History

Hans Heilbronn posed the problem in the 1950s, in the context of combinatorial geometry. He conjectured Delta(n) = O(1/n^2), which seemed natural because n points in a unit square have average spacing 1/sqrt(n) and typical triangle area 1/n. In 1981, Komlós, Pintz and Szemerédi constructed point sets with all triangles of area at least c (log n)/n^2, disproving the conjecture, by placing points on a circle with carefully chosen angles. The upper bound was improved over the years, and the current best upper bound is Delta(n) = O(1/n^(8/7)) up to logarithmic factors, by the work of Komlós, Pintz and Szemerédi and later authors. The gap between (log n)/n^2 and 1/n^(8/7) is the open problem.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the analytic estimates needed are just beyond the reach of current methods. The main term in every approach is understood, and the error term is the whole problem: the available bounds on exponential sums, on the zeta function, and on oscillatory integrals are too weak by a small but decisive amount. Computation supports the claim to high accuracy, but a finite computation is not a demonstration, and the history of analysis contains examples where the numerical evidence was misleading. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The best lower bound is Delta(n) >= c (log n)/n^2, from the Komlós-Pintz-Szemerédi construction using points on a circle. The best upper bound is Delta(n) = O(n^{-8/7}) up to logarithmic factors. The conjecture that Delta(n) is of order 1/n^2 is false. The problem is a central question in combinatorial geometry, and it is related to the problem of the smallest area determined by n points in the plane. No construction achieves Delta(n) larger than (log n)/n^2 by more than a constant factor, and no argument shows Delta(n) must be as small as n^{-8/7}. The true order of Delta(n) is unknown, and the problem is open.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQANS-000014 presents an attempted approach to Heilbronn Triangle Problem, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

Let Delta(n) be the maximum over all sets of n points in the unit square of the minimum area of a triangle determined by three of the points. The trivial bound from pigeonholing is Delta(n) = O(1/n), and the Komlós-Pintz-Szemerédi construction gives Delta(n) >> (log n)/n^2 by placing points on a circle: three points on a circle of radius r have area at most r^2 times the angular gaps, and choosing the angles as a Sidon-like set keeps all areas large. The upper bound uses the fact that n points determine about n^3 triangles, and the sum of the areas of all triangles is bounded, so the minimum cannot be too large; refining this with incidence geometry gives the exponent 8/7. Formally the open question is the asymptotic order of Delta(n) as n tends to infinity.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: For n points in the unit square, what is the largest possible area of the smallest triangle formed by three of them, and how does it behave as n grows? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Heilbronn Triangle Problem would reshape analysis and its neighbours. The problem sits at the junction of harmonic analysis, number theory, geometry and mathematical physics, and the methods developed for it already feed into signal processing, quantum mechanics and the theory of partial differential equations. The zeta function and its relatives control the distribution of the primes, the behaviour of waves, and the spectrum of the Laplacian, so a result here propagates widely. The problem matters because it is a precise statement about a fundamental object, the Fourier transform or the zeta function, and its answer would tell us how far the classical tools of analysis can reach.

Heilbronn check: 12 points on the unit circle, smallest triangle area among triples.
Smallest triangle area: 0.066987
Number of triples: 220
Range limit: one symmetric configuration; the problem asks for the best arrangement for every n.

What this does NOT prove: computing the smallest triangle area among 12 points on a circle, which does not determine the asymptotic order of Delta(n).

  • A3: Vary the worked examples and the historical detail for Heilbronn Triangle Problem.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_heilbronn(n=12):
    pts = [(math.cos(2 * math.pi * k / n), math.sin(2 * math.pi * k / n)) for k in range(n)]
    def area(a, b, c):
        return abs((b[0] - a[0]) * (c[1] - a[1]) - (c[0] - a[0]) * (b[1] - a[1])) / 2.0
    mn = min(area(pts[i], pts[j], pts[k]) for i in range(n) for j in range(i + 1, n) for k in range(j + 1, n))
    return "\n".join([
        "Heilbronn check: %d points on the unit circle, smallest triangle area among triples." % n,
        "Smallest triangle area: %.6f" % mn,
        "Number of triples: %d" % (n * (n - 1) * (n - 2) // 6),
        "Range limit: one symmetric configuration; the problem asks for the best arrangement for every n.",
    ])

payload = eng_heilbronn()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Heilbronn Triangle Problem")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: computing the smallest triangle area among 12 points on a circle, which does not determine the asymptotic order of Delta(n)")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Sphere Packing in Higher Dimensions JAH-USEQAN-000015 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The sphere packing problem asks for the densest arrangement of equal spheres in n-dimensional space. In dimensions 1, 2 and 3 the answer is known: the hexagonal packing in the plane and the face-centred cubic packing in space, the latter proved by Hales in 1998 as the Kepler conjecture. In dimensions 8 and 24 the answer was found by Viazovska in 2016 and by Cohn, Kumar, Miller, Radchenko and Viazovska in 2017: the E8 lattice and the Leech lattice are optimal. In every other dimension the densest packing is unknown. The problem is analytic in flavour because the modern method, the Cohn-Elkies linear programming bound, uses the Fourier transform of a function to bound the density, and the bound is sharp only in dimensions 8 and 24.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: What is the densest packing of equal spheres in R^n for dimensions n other than 1, 2, 3, 8 and 24? The sphere packing problem asks for the densest arrangement of equal spheres in n-dimensional space. In dimensions 1, 2 and 3 the answer is known: the hexagonal packing in the plane and the face-centred cubic packing in space, the latter proved by Hales in 1998 as the Kepler conjecture. In dimensions 8 and 24 the answer was found by Viazovska in 2016 and by Cohn, Kumar, Miller, Radchenko and Viazovska in 2017: the E8 lattice and the Leech lattice are optimal. In every other dimension the densest packing is unknown. The problem is analytic in flavour because the modern method, the Cohn-Elkies linear programming bound, uses the Fourier transform of a function to bound the density, and the bound is sharp only in dimensions 8 and 24.

History

Kepler conjectured in 1611 that the face-centred cubic packing is densest in three dimensions, and the problem took until 1998 to settle, when Hales gave a computer-assisted demonstration. The linear programming bound of Cohn and Elkies, introduced in 2003, gave a powerful analytic method: if a function and its Fourier transform satisfy certain sign conditions, they bound the packing density. Viazovska found the magic function for dimension 8 in 2016, using modular forms, and the same method worked in dimension 24. The method fails in other dimensions because the required magic function does not exist, as shown by the work of Cohn and others. The densest packings in dimensions 4, 5, 6, 7 and above 24 are unknown.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the analytic estimates needed are just beyond the reach of current methods. The main term in every approach is understood, and the error term is the whole problem: the available bounds on exponential sums, on the zeta function, and on oscillatory integrals are too weak by a small but decisive amount. Computation supports the claim to high accuracy, but a finite computation is not a demonstration, and the history of analysis contains examples where the numerical evidence was misleading. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The densest sphere packings are known in dimensions 1, 2, 3, 8 and 24. In dimension 8 the E8 lattice has density pi^4/384, about 0.2537, and in dimension 24 the Leech lattice has density pi^12/12!, about 0.00193. In other dimensions only bounds are known: the Cohn-Elkies linear programming bound gives upper bounds, and explicit lattice packings give lower bounds, and the gap between them is large. In dimension 4, the D4 lattice is conjectured to be optimal but not proved. The problem is open in every dimension except 1, 2, 3, 8 and 24, and it is one of the central problems in discrete geometry and harmonic analysis.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQANS-000015 presents an attempted approach to Sphere Packing in Higher Dimensions, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

The density of a sphere packing is the fraction of space covered by the spheres. The Cohn-Elkies bound says that if f is a Schwartz function with f(0) = 1, f(x) <= 0 for |x| >= 1, and its Fourier transform f-hat >= 0 everywhere, then the packing density is at most f-hat(0)/2^n. The bound is proved by Poisson summation over a lattice. In dimensions 8 and 24, Viazovska and coauthors constructed functions attaining the bound, using the theory of modular forms to build a function with the right signs. In other dimensions the bound is not sharp, and no function attains it. The linear programming bound is the analytic heart of the problem, and its failure in other dimensions is itself a theorem. Formally: the optimal density in R^n is unknown for n not in {1, 2, 3, 8, 24}.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: What is the densest packing of equal spheres in R^n for dimensions n other than 1, 2, 3, 8 and 24? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Sphere Packing in Higher Dimensions would reshape analysis and its neighbours. The problem sits at the junction of harmonic analysis, number theory, geometry and mathematical physics, and the methods developed for it already feed into signal processing, quantum mechanics and the theory of partial differential equations. The zeta function and its relatives control the distribution of the primes, the behaviour of waves, and the spectrum of the Laplacian, so a result here propagates widely. The problem matters because it is a precise statement about a fundamental object, the Fourier transform or the zeta function, and its answer would tell us how far the classical tools of analysis can reach.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_sphere_pack(r=8):
    # Hexagonal lattice packing density in the plane: count lattice points of the
    # triangular lattice inside a large disk and compare with pi/sqrt(12).
    import math as _m
    cnt = 0
    pts = 0
    for i in range(-r, r + 1):
        for j in range(-r, r + 1):
            x = i + 0.5 * j
            y = _m.sqrt(3) / 2 * j
            pts += 1
            if x * x + y * y <= r * r:
                cnt += 1
    area = _m.pi * r * r
    cell = _m.sqrt(3) / 2
    return "\n".join([
        "Sphere packing check: triangular lattice points inside a disk of radius %d." % r,
        "Lattice points inside the disk: %d" % cnt,
        "Disk area: %.2f, lattice cell area: %.4f" % (area, cell),
        "Estimated packing density: %.4f (hexagonal density pi/sqrt(12) = %.4f)" % (cnt * cell / area, _m.pi / _m.sqrt(12)),
        "Range limit: the plane only; sphere packing in higher dimensions is the open problem.",
    ])

payload = eng_sphere_pack()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Sphere Packing in Higher Dimensions")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: estimating the hexagonal packing density in the plane from lattice points, which does not address packings in higher dimensions")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False

            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)

    return sieve(n)

        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):

    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_sphere_pack(r=8):
    # Hexagonal lattice packing density in the plane: count lattice points of the
    # triangular lattice inside a large disk and compare with pi/sqrt(12).
    import math as _m
    cnt = 0
    pts = 0
    for i in range(-r, r + 1):
        for j in range(-r, r + 1):
            x = i + 0.5 * j
            y = _m.sqrt(3) / 2 * j
            pts += 1
            if x * x + y * y <= r * r:
                cnt += 1
    area = _m.pi * r * r
    cell = _m.sqrt(3) / 2
    return "\n".join([
        "Sphere packing check: triangular lattice points inside a disk of radius %d." % r,
        "Lattice points inside the disk: %d" % cnt,
        "Disk area: %.2f, lattice cell area: %.4f" % (area, cell),
        "Estimated packing density: %.4f (hexagonal density pi/sqrt(12) = %.4f)" % (cnt * cell / area, _m.pi / _m.sqrt(12)),
        "Range limit: the plane only; sphere packing in higher dimensions is the open problem.",
    ])

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Sphere Packing in Higher Dimensions")
    print("Edition: public")
    print("STATUS: OPEN")
    print("Statement: What is the densest packing of equal spheres in R^n for dimensions n other than 1, 2, 3, 8 and 24?")
    payload = eng_sphere_pack()
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: estimating the hexagonal packing density in the plane from lattice points, which does not address packings in higher dimensions")
    print("Attempt summary: this public record carries no solving attempt; see the Signature mirror record.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Selberg Class Conjectures JAH-USEQAN-000016 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The Selberg class is an axiomatic class of Dirichlet series that are expected to be exactly the L-functions of arithmetic interest: the Riemann zeta function, Dirichlet L-functions, and the L-functions of automorphic forms. Selberg defined the class in 1989 by four axioms: a Dirichlet series with an Euler product, a functional equation, a Ramanujan bound on the coefficients, and analytic continuation. He conjectured that the class has strong properties: the orthonormality conjecture, which says distinct primitive elements are orthogonal in a precise sense; that every element factors uniquely into primitive elements; and that the class is closed under the operations that automorphic L-functions satisfy. All of these conjectures are open, and they are the framework for the modern theory of L-functions.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: Do the Selberg class axioms imply the Selberg orthonormality conjecture and the other conjectures about L-functions: that the class is closed under the expected operations and its elements are the automorphic L-functions? The Selberg class is an axiomatic class of Dirichlet series that are expected to be exactly the L-functions of arithmetic interest: the Riemann zeta function, Dirichlet L-functions, and the L-functions of automorphic forms. Selberg defined the class in 1989 by four axioms: a Dirichlet series with an Euler product, a functional equation, a Ramanujan bound on the coefficients, and analytic continuation. He conjectured that the class has strong properties: the orthonormality conjecture, which says distinct primitive elements are orthogonal in a precise sense; that every element factors uniquely into primitive elements; and that the class is closed under the operations that automorphic L-functions satisfy. All of these conjectures are open, and they are the framework for the modern theory of L-functions.

History

Atle Selberg introduced the class in 1989 in a paper on old and new conjectures about the zeta function, as an attempt to axiomatise the L-functions that occur in number theory. The conjectures were motivated by the Langlands program, which predicts that all L-functions come from automorphic forms. In the following decades, the theory of the Selberg class was developed by Conrey, Ghosh, Kaczorowski, Perelli and others, who proved some of the conjectures under additional hypotheses and showed that the class has a rich structure. The orthonormality conjecture remains open, and it is the key to showing that the Selberg class is exactly the class of automorphic L-functions. The conjectures are central to the Langlands program.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the analytic estimates needed are just beyond the reach of current methods. The main term in every approach is understood, and the error term is the whole problem: the available bounds on exponential sums, on the zeta function, and on oscillatory integrals are too weak by a small but decisive amount. Computation supports the claim to high accuracy, but a finite computation is not a demonstration, and the history of analysis contains examples where the numerical evidence was misleading. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The Selberg class is known to contain the Riemann zeta function, all Dirichlet L-functions, and the L-functions of holomorphic modular forms. The orthonormality conjecture is open: it says that for distinct primitive F and G in the class, the sum over primes of a_F(p) times the conjugate of a_G(p) divided by p is o(log log x), while for F = G it is log log x. The unique factorisation into primitive elements is known under the assumption of the orthonormality conjecture, and it is open unconditionally. The Selberg conjectures imply the Ramanujan conjecture for GL(n) in a weak form, and they are the standard framework for stating conjectures about L-functions. No counterexample to any of the conjectures is known.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQANS-000016 presents an attempted approach to Selberg Class Conjectures, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

An element of the Selberg class is a Dirichlet series F(s) = sum a(n) n^{-s} with an Euler product, a functional equation relating F(s) and F(1-s), the Ramanujan bound a(n) << n^epsilon, and analytic continuation to the whole plane except possibly a pole at s = 1. The degree of F is defined by the functional equation, and F is primitive if it cannot be written as a product of two elements of smaller degree. The orthonormality conjecture is the statement that the prime coefficients of distinct primitive elements are orthogonal: sum_{p <= x} a_F(p) conj(a_G(p))/p = delta_{F,G} log log x + O(1). This would give a unique factorisation theory for L-functions, analogous to the factorisation of integers into primes. Formally the conjectures are a package about the structure of the class, and they are all open.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: Do the Selberg class axioms imply the Selberg orthonormality conjecture and the other conjectures about L-functions: that the class is closed under the expected operations and its elements are the automorphic L-functions? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Selberg Class Conjectures would reshape analysis and its neighbours. The problem sits at the junction of harmonic analysis, number theory, geometry and mathematical physics, and the methods developed for it already feed into signal processing, quantum mechanics and the theory of partial differential equations. The zeta function and its relatives control the distribution of the primes, the behaviour of waves, and the spectrum of the Laplacian, so a result here propagates widely. The problem matters because it is a precise statement about a fundamental object, the Fourier transform or the zeta function, and its answer would tell us how far the classical tools of analysis can reach.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_selberg_li(xs=(1000, 10000, 100000)):
    def li(x):
        # li(x) by Simpson on 1/ln t from 2 to x
        N = 2000
        h = (x - 2.0) / N
        s = 1.0 / math.log(2.0) + 1.0 / math.log(x)
        for i in range(1, N):
            w = 4 if i % 2 == 1 else 2
            s += w / math.log(2.0 + i * h)
        return s * h / 3.0
    lines = ["Selberg class check: pi(x) against li(x) (prime number theorem scale)."]
    for x in xs:
        px = len(primes_upto(x))
        lx = li(x)
        lines.append("x=%d: pi(x) = %d, li(x) = %.1f, difference = %.1f" % (x, px, lx, px - lx))
    lines.append("Range limit: three values of x; the Selberg class axioms concern all L-functions.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

payload = eng_selberg_li()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Selberg Class Conjectures")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: comparing pi(x) with li(x) at three values of x, which does not establish any Selberg class conjecture")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Normality of Pi JAH-USEQAN-000017 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The normality of pi asks whether the decimal expansion of pi is normal: every block of k digits occurs with frequency 10^{-k}, so each digit 0 through 9 occurs one tenth of the time, each pair of digits occurs one hundredth of the time, and so on. The same question is asked in every base. The digits of pi computed so far, trillions of them, look perfectly random and pass every statistical test, but no argument shows that the pattern continues. It is not even known whether the digit 7 occurs infinitely often in the decimal expansion of pi, although it surely does. The problem is one of the most embarrassing open questions in mathematics: a number computed to trillions of digits, whose most basic statistical property is unknown.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: Is pi a normal number: does every finite string of digits occur in its decimal expansion with the expected frequency? The normality of pi asks whether the decimal expansion of pi is normal: every block of k digits occurs with frequency 10^{-k}, so each digit 0 through 9 occurs one tenth of the time, each pair of digits occurs one hundredth of the time, and so on. The same question is asked in every base. The digits of pi computed so far, trillions of them, look perfectly random and pass every statistical test, but no argument shows that the pattern continues. It is not even known whether the digit 7 occurs infinitely often in the decimal expansion of pi, although it surely does. The problem is one of the most embarrassing open questions in mathematics: a number computed to trillions of digits, whose most basic statistical property is unknown.

History

Émile Borel introduced the concept of a normal number in 1909 and proved that almost all real numbers are normal, in the sense of Lebesgue measure. The question of whether any specific familiar constant is normal has resisted all attempts: it is not known whether pi, e, sqrt(2) or log 2 is normal in any base. The computation of pi advanced from millions of digits in the twentieth century to trillions of digits today, and the digits pass every statistical test for randomness. In 2016, work by Bailey, Borwein and others connected the normality of pi with the theory of pseudorandom number generators, but no demonstration appeared. The problem remains open, and it is considered one of the hardest questions about the decimal expansion of a constant.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the analytic estimates needed are just beyond the reach of current methods. The main term in every approach is understood, and the error term is the whole problem: the available bounds on exponential sums, on the zeta function, and on oscillatory integrals are too weak by a small but decisive amount. Computation supports the claim to high accuracy, but a finite computation is not a demonstration, and the history of analysis contains examples where the numerical evidence was misleading. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

Pi has been computed to more than 10^14 digits, and the digit frequencies match the normal distribution to within statistical error. It is not known whether pi is normal in any base, and it is not known whether any single digit occurs infinitely often in its expansion. Almost all numbers are normal, by Borel's theorem, so pi is expected to be normal, but the expectation is not a demonstration. The related question of whether pi is simply normal, meaning each digit occurs with frequency 1/10, is also open. The normality of e and of sqrt(2) are equally open. No argument is known that would show the digits of any naturally occurring irrational constant are equidistributed.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQANS-000017 presents an attempted approach to Normality of Pi, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

A real number x is normal in base b if, for every k, every block of k digits occurs in the base-b expansion of x with frequency b^{-k}. Borel's theorem says almost all numbers are normal, by the strong law of large numbers applied to the digits, which behave like independent uniform random variables for a typical number. To show a specific number is normal, one would need to control the digits, and the digits of pi are defined by the analytic formula for pi, not by a digit-generating process. The Bailey-Borwein-Plouffe formula gives a way to compute individual hexadecimal digits of pi without computing the earlier ones, but it does not give statistical control. Formally: for every k and every block B of k decimal digits, the frequency of B in the decimal expansion of pi is 10^{-k}.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: Is pi a normal number: does every finite string of digits occur in its decimal expansion with the expected frequency? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Normality of Pi would reshape analysis and its neighbours. The problem sits at the junction of harmonic analysis, number theory, geometry and mathematical physics, and the methods developed for it already feed into signal processing, quantum mechanics and the theory of partial differential equations. The zeta function and its relatives control the distribution of the primes, the behaviour of waves, and the spectrum of the Laplacian, so a result here propagates widely. The problem matters because it is a precise statement about a fundamental object, the Fourier transform or the zeta function, and its answer would tell us how far the classical tools of analysis can reach.

Demo

The demo computes a verified partial result and states what it does not establish. It runs the computation twice, prints the SHA-256 digest of each run, and reports whether the digests match. Its output is:

Normality check: first 200 digits of pi computed by the Machin formula (Decimal arithmetic).
Digit counts 0..9: [19, 20, 24, 20, 22, 20, 16, 12, 25, 23]
Expected count per digit: 20.0
Largest deviation from expected: 8
Range limit: 200 digits only; normality of pi is about the infinite digit sequence.

What this does NOT prove: counting digit frequencies in the first 200 digits of pi, which does not establish normality.
