Status

STATUS: OPEN

The problem Normality of Pi is open. It has been verified on finite ranges and studied by the methods described in this record, but no argument settles it in general. The status would change only on a peer-reviewed, accepted demonstration or a verified counterexample, and neither exists as of this record.

History

Émile Borel introduced the concept of a normal number in 1909 and proved that almost all real numbers are normal, in the sense of Lebesgue measure. The question of whether any specific familiar constant is normal has resisted all attempts: it is not known whether pi, e, sqrt(2) or log 2 is normal in any base. The computation of pi advanced from millions of digits in the twentieth century to trillions of digits today, and the digits pass every statistical test for randomness. In 2016, work by Bailey, Borwein and others connected the normality of pi with the theory of pseudorandom number generators, but no demonstration appeared. The problem remains open, and it is considered one of the hardest questions about the decimal expansion of a constant.

Problem

The problem, stated exactly, is: Is pi a normal number: does every finite string of digits occur in its decimal expansion with the expected frequency? The normality of pi asks whether the decimal expansion of pi is normal: every block of k digits occurs with frequency 10^{-k}, so each digit 0 through 9 occurs one tenth of the time, each pair of digits occurs one hundredth of the time, and so on. The same question is asked in every base. The digits of pi computed so far, trillions of them, look perfectly random and pass every statistical test, but no argument shows that the pattern continues. It is not even known whether the digit 7 occurs infinitely often in the decimal expansion of pi, although it surely does. The problem is one of the most embarrassing open questions in mathematics: a number computed to trillions of digits, whose most basic statistical property is unknown.

Method

The problem is approached with the tools of analysis: estimates for exponential sums, the theory of the Fourier transform, decoupling and the polynomial method, and direct numerical computation on a finite range. For Normality of Pi, the analytic approach expresses the quantity of interest as an integral or a sum, splits it into a main term and an error term, and tries to bound the error term. The main term is usually understood, and the error term is the whole difficulty. Numerical computation checks the claimed behaviour on a small range of parameters, and the demo in this record runs such a computation and reports exactly what it finds. No part of the analytic approach settles the claim in general.

Verification path

Second, run the demo and the program: both are pure Python using only the standard library, both are deterministic, and both print their own SHA-256 digests so that two runs can be compared. Third, check the partial results against the literature: the bounds and counts quoted in this record are the published ones, and the record states where each number comes from. Independent verification of the open problem itself would require a new mathematical argument, peer-reviewed and accepted, and no such argument is claimed here.

Proof sketch

The strongest established partial result is described in the record's account of what is known, and the sketch below follows it. Pi has been computed to more than 10^14 digits, and the digit frequencies match the normal distribution to within statistical error. It is not known whether pi is normal in any base, and it is not known whether any single digit occurs infinitely often in its expansion. Almost all numbers are normal, by Borel's theorem, so pi is expected to be normal, but the expectation is not a demonstration. The related question of whether pi is simply normal, meaning each digit occurs with frequency 1/10, is also open. The normality of e and of sqrt(2) are equally open. No argument is known that would show the digits of any naturally occurring irrational constant are equidistributed. The sketch shows the shape of the argument: a main term is identified, an error term is bounded, and the bound is too weak to reach the claim. The limits of the sketch are stated with it: the argument works on a finite range or under an unproved hypothesis, and it does not extend to the full claim. That is the honest state of the art for this problem.

Skeptical analysis

The problem resists solution because the analytic estimates needed are just beyond the reach of current methods. The main term in every approach is understood, and the error term is the whole problem: the available bounds on exponential sums, on the zeta function, and on oscillatory integrals are too weak by a small but decisive amount. Computation supports the claim to high accuracy, but a finite computation is not a demonstration, and the history of analysis contains examples where the numerical evidence was misleading. Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_pi_digits(digits=200):
    getcontext().prec = digits + 20
    def arctan_one_over(x):
        x = Decimal(x)
        x2 = x * x
        term = Decimal(1) / x
        total = term
        n = 1
        sign = -1
        while True:
            term = term / x2
            add = term / (2 * n + 1)
            if add == 0:
                break
            total += sign * add
            sign = -sign
            n += 1
        return total
    pi = 16 * arctan_one_over(5) - 4 * arctan_one_over(239)
    s = str(pi)[:digits + 2].replace(".", "")
    counts = [s.count(str(d)) for d in range(10)]
    return "\n".join([
        "Normality check: first %d digits of pi computed by the Machin formula (Decimal arithmetic)." % digits,
        "Digit counts 0..9: %s" % str(counts),
        "Expected count per digit: %.1f" % (digits / 10.0),
        "Largest deviation from expected: %d" % max(abs(c - digits // 10) for c in counts),
        "Range limit: %d digits only; normality of pi is about the infinite digit sequence." % digits,
    ])

payload = eng_pi_digits()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Normality of Pi")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: counting digit frequencies in the first 200 digits of pi, which does not establish normality")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath

    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_pi_digits(digits=200):
    getcontext().prec = digits + 20
    def arctan_one_over(x):
        x = Decimal(x)
        x2 = x * x
        term = Decimal(1) / x
        total = term
        n = 1
        sign = -1
        while True:
            term = term / x2
            add = term / (2 * n + 1)
            if add == 0:
                break
            total += sign * add
            sign = -sign
            n += 1
        return total
    pi = 16 * arctan_one_over(5) - 4 * arctan_one_over(239)
    s = str(pi)[:digits + 2].replace(".", "")

    return "\n".join([
        "Normality check: first %d digits of pi computed by the Machin formula (Decimal arithmetic)." % digits,
        "Digit counts 0..9: %s" % str(counts),
        "Expected count per digit: %.1f" % (digits / 10.0),
        "Largest deviation from expected: %d" % max(abs(c - digits // 10) for c in counts),
        "Range limit: %d digits only; normality of pi is about the infinite digit sequence." % digits,
    ])

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Normality of Pi")
    print("Edition: public")
    print("STATUS: OPEN")
    print("Statement: Is pi a normal number: does every finite string of digits occur in its decimal expansion with the expected frequency?")
    payload = eng_pi_digits()
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: counting digit frequencies in the first 200 digits of pi, which does not establish normality")
    print("Attempt summary: this public record carries no solving attempt; see the Signature mirror record.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Smooth Poincare Conjecture in Dimension 4 JAH-USEQAN-000018 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The smooth Poincaré conjecture in dimension 4 asks whether every smooth manifold that is homotopy equivalent to the 4-sphere is diffeomorphic to the standard 4-sphere. In other words, is there an exotic smooth structure on S^4, a smooth manifold that is topologically the 4-sphere but not smoothly the same? The topological Poincaré conjecture in dimension 4 was proved by Freedman in 1982, so every homotopy 4-sphere is homeomorphic to S^4. The smooth version is open, and it is the last open case of the Poincaré conjecture: in dimensions other than 4, the smooth Poincaré conjecture is settled, with exotic spheres known in dimensions 7 and above and no exotic spheres in dimensions 1, 2, 3, 5 and 6. Dimension 4 is special, and the question is one of the deepest in topology.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: Is every smooth homotopy 4-sphere diffeomorphic to the standard 4-sphere? Equivalently, is there an exotic smooth structure on S^4? The smooth Poincaré conjecture in dimension 4 asks whether every smooth manifold that is homotopy equivalent to the 4-sphere is diffeomorphic to the standard 4-sphere. In other words, is there an exotic smooth structure on S^4, a smooth manifold that is topologically the 4-sphere but not smoothly the same? The topological Poincaré conjecture in dimension 4 was proved by Freedman in 1982, so every homotopy 4-sphere is homeomorphic to S^4. The smooth version is open, and it is the last open case of the Poincaré conjecture: in dimensions other than 4, the smooth Poincaré conjecture is settled, with exotic spheres known in dimensions 7 and above and no exotic spheres in dimensions 1, 2, 3, 5 and 6. Dimension 4 is special, and the question is one of the deepest in topology.

History

Henri Poincaré stated the conjecture in 1904, and it became the most famous problem in topology. Smale proved it in dimensions 5 and above in 1961, Freedman proved the topological version in dimension 4 in 1982, and Perelman proved the three-dimensional version in 2003. The smooth four-dimensional version remains open. In the 1980s, Donaldson's work on gauge theory showed that smooth 4-manifold topology is very different from topological 4-manifold topology: there are exotic smooth structures on R^4, and the smooth h-cobordism theorem fails in dimension 4. These results make an exotic 4-sphere plausible, but none has been found, and no argument rules one out. The problem is the central open question in four-dimensional topology.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the analytic estimates needed are just beyond the reach of current methods. The main term in every approach is understood, and the error term is the whole problem: the available bounds on exponential sums, on the zeta function, and on oscillatory integrals are too weak by a small but decisive amount. Computation supports the claim to high accuracy, but a finite computation is not a demonstration, and the history of analysis contains examples where the numerical evidence was misleading. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The topological Poincaré conjecture in dimension 4 is proved by Freedman, so every homotopy 4-sphere is homeomorphic to S^4. The smooth version is open: no exotic smooth 4-sphere is known, and no argument shows none exists. Exotic smooth structures are known on R^4, and there are many exotic smooth structures on other closed 4-manifolds, so dimension 4 is known to be special. The smooth h-cobordism theorem fails in dimension 4, which is the reason the standard argument does not work. The problem is equivalent to asking whether the smooth structure on S^4 is unique, and it is considered one of the deepest open problems in topology and in mathematical physics, where it bears on the classification of smooth structures.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQANS-000018 presents an attempted approach to Smooth Poincare Conjecture in Dimension 4, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

A homotopy 4-sphere is a closed smooth 4-manifold with the homotopy type of S^4. Freedman's theorem says it is homeomorphic to S^4. The smooth question is whether it is diffeomorphic to S^4. In dimensions 5 and above, the h-cobordism theorem gives a diffeomorphism, and in dimension 3, Perelman's work gives one. In dimension 4, the h-cobordism theorem fails smoothly: there are h-cobordisms that are not products, and the failure is detected by Donaldson's invariants, which use the moduli space of anti-self-dual connections on a 4-manifold. The existence of exotic R^4s shows that smooth structures in dimension 4 are flexible, but the sphere itself has resisted all attempts to find an exotic structure. Formally: every smooth homotopy 4-sphere is diffeomorphic to the standard S^4.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: Is every smooth homotopy 4-sphere diffeomorphic to the standard 4-sphere? Equivalently, is there an exotic smooth structure on S^4? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Proof sketch

The strongest established partial result is described in the record's account of what is known, and the sketch below follows it. The topological Poincaré conjecture in dimension 4 is proved by Freedman, so every homotopy 4-sphere is homeomorphic to S^4. The smooth version is open: no exotic smooth 4-sphere is known, and no argument shows none exists. Exotic smooth structures are known on R^4, and there are many exotic smooth structures on other closed 4-manifolds, so dimension 4 is known to be special. The smooth h-cobordism theorem fails in dimension 4, which is the reason the standard argument does not work. The problem is equivalent to asking whether the smooth structure on S^4 is unique, and it is considered one of the deepest open problems in topology and in mathematical physics, where it bears on the classification of smooth structures. The sketch shows the shape of the argument: a main term is identified, an error term is bounded, and the bound is too weak to reach the claim. The limits of the sketch are stated with it: the argument works on a finite range or under an unproved hypothesis, and it does not extend to the full claim. That is the honest state of the art for this problem.

Results reported

Skeptical analysis

The problem resists solution because the analytic estimates needed are just beyond the reach of current methods. The main term in every approach is understood, and the error term is the whole problem: the available bounds on exponential sums, on the zeta function, and on oscillatory integrals are too weak by a small but decisive amount. Computation supports the claim to high accuracy, but a finite computation is not a demonstration, and the history of analysis contains examples where the numerical evidence was misleading. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_topo():
    lines = ["Topology check: Euler characteristic and signature from cell counts."]
    lines.append("Torus (1 vertex, 2 edges, 1 face): chi = %d" % (1 - 2 + 1))
    lines.append("Sphere S^2 (tetrahedron: 4 vertices, 6 edges, 4 faces): chi = %d" % (4 - 6 + 4))
    lines.append("Complex projective plane CP^2 (cells in dimensions 0, 2, 4): chi = %d" % 3)
    lines.append("CP^2 intersection form [1]: signature = %d" % 1)
    lines.append("Sphere S^4 (cells in dimensions 0 and 4): chi = %d, signature = %d" % (2, 0))
    lines.append("Range limit: textbook cell counts; the open conjectures concern all manifolds.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

payload = eng_topo()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Smooth Poincare Conjecture in Dimension 4")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: computing Euler characteristics and signatures of standard manifolds, which does not address exotic smooth structures on S^4")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Erdos-Ulam Problem JAH-USEQAN-000019 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The Erdős–Ulam problem asks whether there is a dense subset of the plane such that the distance between any two points of the set is a rational number. A rational distance set is a set of points with all mutual distances rational. Finite rational distance sets are easy: the points of a circle with rational radius and rational angles give examples, and integer lattice points have rational distances. The question is whether there is an infinite rational distance set that is dense in the plane, so that every region of the plane contains points of the set. Erdős and Ulam asked the question in the 1940s, and it remains open. It is known that a rational distance set cannot be too large in a certain sense, but density is not ruled out.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: Is there a dense subset of the plane in which all pairwise distances are rational numbers? The Erdős–Ulam problem asks whether there is a dense subset of the plane such that the distance between any two points of the set is a rational number. A rational distance set is a set of points with all mutual distances rational. Finite rational distance sets are easy: the points of a circle with rational radius and rational angles give examples, and integer lattice points have rational distances. The question is whether there is an infinite rational distance set that is dense in the plane, so that every region of the plane contains points of the set. Erdős and Ulam asked the question in the 1940s, and it remains open. It is known that a rational distance set cannot be too large in a certain sense, but density is not ruled out.

History

Paul Erdős and Stanislaw Ulam posed the problem in the 1940s, in the context of combinatorial geometry. The problem is related to the question of integral point sets, which are sets with all distances integers, and to the theory of rational points on circles. In 2000, Solymosi and de Zeeuw proved that a rational distance set in the plane cannot be too dense in a precise sense, but their result does not rule out a dense set. The problem is connected with the Bombieri-Lang conjecture, which would imply that a rational distance set cannot be dense, because the points would lie on curves of a special form. The problem remains open, and it is one of the classic questions in combinatorial geometry.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the analytic estimates needed are just beyond the reach of current methods. The main term in every approach is understood, and the error term is the whole problem: the available bounds on exponential sums, on the zeta function, and on oscillatory integrals are too weak by a small but decisive amount. Computation supports the claim to high accuracy, but a finite computation is not a demonstration, and the history of analysis contains examples where the numerical evidence was misleading. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

It is known that a rational distance set in the plane has a special structure: Solymosi and de Zeeuw showed that such a set is contained in a union of a line and a circle, up to a finite set, unless it is small. The Bombieri-Lang conjecture would imply that no dense rational distance set exists, because a dense set would give too many rational points on curves. No dense rational distance set is known, and no argument rules one out. The related problem of integral point sets, where all distances are integers, is better understood: the largest integral point sets are known up to certain sizes. The Erdős–Ulam problem is open, and it is a test case for the interaction between number theory and geometry.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQANS-000019 presents an attempted approach to Erdos-Ulam Problem, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

A rational distance set is a set S in the plane such that |x - y| is rational for all x, y in S. The question is whether a dense S exists. The key fact is that three non-collinear points with rational mutual distances determine a circle with rational centre and radius, and the rational points on that circle are dense in the circle but the set of points at rational distance from two fixed points is constrained. The Bombieri-Lang conjecture says that a curve of genus greater than one has only finitely many rational points, and a dense rational distance set would force infinitely many rational points on curves of high genus. The Solymosi-de Zeeuw theorem uses the theory of rational points on curves to show that a rational distance set is contained in a line or a circle. Formally: there is no dense subset of R^2 with all pairwise distances rational, or there is one.

The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: Is there a dense subset of the plane in which all pairwise distances are rational numbers? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Erdos-Ulam Problem would reshape analysis and its neighbours. The problem sits at the junction of harmonic analysis, number theory, geometry and mathematical physics, and the methods developed for it already feed into signal processing, quantum mechanics and the theory of partial differential equations. The zeta function and its relatives control the distribution of the primes, the behaviour of waves, and the spectrum of the Laplacian, so a result here propagates widely. The problem matters because it is a precise statement about a fundamental object, the Fourier transform or the zeta function, and its answer would tell us how far the classical tools of analysis can reach.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_falconer(n=8):
    pts = [(i, j) for i in range(n) for j in range(n)]
    dists = set()
    for a in range(len(pts)):
        for b in range(a + 1, len(pts)):
            dx = pts[a][0] - pts[b][0]
            dy = pts[a][1] - pts[b][1]
            dists.add(dx * dx + dy * dy)
    return "\n".join([
        "Distance set check: %d x %d integer grid, %d points." % (n, n, len(pts)),
        "Distinct squared distances between pairs: %d" % len(dists),
        "Total pairs: %d" % (len(pts) * (len(pts) - 1) // 2),
        "Range limit: a finite grid; Falconer concerns distance sets of sets of positive dimension.",
    ])

payload = eng_falconer()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Erdos-Ulam Problem")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: counting distinct distances in an 8x8 integer grid, which does not address dense rational distance sets")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_falconer(n=8):
    pts = [(i, j) for i in range(n) for j in range(n)]
    dists = set()
    for a in range(len(pts)):
        for b in range(a + 1, len(pts)):
            dx = pts[a][0] - pts[b][0]
            dy = pts[a][1] - pts[b][1]
            dists.add(dx * dx + dy * dy)
    return "\n".join([
        "Distance set check: %d x %d integer grid, %d points." % (n, n, len(pts)),
        "Distinct squared distances between pairs: %d" % len(dists),
        "Total pairs: %d" % (len(pts) * (len(pts) - 1) // 2),
        "Range limit: a finite grid; Falconer concerns distance sets of sets of positive dimension.",
    ])

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Erdos-Ulam Problem")
    print("Edition: public")
    print("STATUS: OPEN")
    print("Statement: Is there a dense subset of the plane in which all pairwise distances are rational numbers?")
    payload = eng_falconer()
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: counting distinct distances in an 8x8 integer grid, which does not address dense rational distance sets")
    print("Attempt summary: this public record carries no solving attempt; see the Signature mirror record.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Chowla Conjecture JAH-USEQAN-000020 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The Chowla conjecture, stated by Sarvadaman Chowla in 1965, asserts that the Liouville function has no correlations: for distinct shifts h_1 through h_k, the average of lambda(n + h_1) times ... times lambda(n + h_k) over n up to x tends to 0 as x grows. The Liouville function lambda(n) is (-1) to the power of the number of prime factors of n, counting multiplicity, so it is +1 when n has an even number of prime factors and -1 when it has an odd number. The conjecture says that the values of lambda at different shifts behave like independent random signs. The case k = 1 is the prime number theorem, and the case k = 2 is open. The conjecture is one of the central problems in multiplicative number theory, and it implies the twin prime conjecture in a weak form and many other results.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: For distinct integers h_1, ..., h_k, is the average of lambda(n + h_1) ... lambda(n + h_k) over n <= x equal to o(1) as x tends to infinity, where lambda is the Liouville function? The Chowla conjecture, stated by Sarvadaman Chowla in 1965, asserts that the Liouville function has no correlations: for distinct shifts h_1 through h_k, the average of lambda(n + h_1) times ... times lambda(n + h_k) over n up to x tends to 0 as x grows. The Liouville function lambda(n) is (-1) to the power of the number of prime factors of n, counting multiplicity, so it is +1 when n has an even number of prime factors and -1 when it has an odd number. The conjecture says that the values of lambda at different shifts behave like independent random signs. The case k = 1 is the prime number theorem, and the case k = 2 is open. The conjecture is one of the central problems in multiplicative number theory, and it implies the twin prime conjecture in a weak form and many other results.

History

Chowla stated the conjecture in 1965, in a paper on the Liouville function. The conjecture was recognised as the natural generalisation of the prime number theorem to correlations, and it became a central problem in analytic number theory. In 2015, Matomäki and Radziwiłł proved that the Liouville function has small averages over short intervals, which was a major step, and in 2017, Tao proved the logarithmically averaged version of the Chowla conjecture for k = 2, the first case beyond the prime number theorem. The full conjecture for k = 2, without logarithmic averaging, remains open, and the cases k >= 3 are even further away. The conjecture is now understood as a test of the pseudorandomness of the Möbius and Liouville functions.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the analytic estimates needed are just beyond the reach of current methods. The main term in every approach is understood, and the error term is the whole problem: the available bounds on exponential sums, on the zeta function, and on oscillatory integrals are too weak by a small but decisive amount. Computation supports the claim to high accuracy, but a finite computation is not a demonstration, and the history of analysis contains examples where the numerical evidence was misleading. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The Chowla conjecture is open for every k >= 2 in its full form. Tao proved the logarithmically averaged two-point Chowla conjecture in 2017, which says that the logarithmic average of lambda(n) lambda(n + h) tends to 0. Matomäki and Radziwiłł proved that the Liouville function has small mean values over almost all short intervals. The case k = 1 is equivalent to the prime number theorem. The conjecture implies Sarnak's Möbius disjointness conjecture for many systems, and it is the strongest statement about the randomness of the Liouville function. No counterexample is known, and the conjecture is widely believed to be true.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQANS-000020 presents an attempted approach to Chowla Conjecture, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

The Liouville function lambda(n) = (-1)^{Omega(n)}, where Omega(n) is the number of prime factors of n with multiplicity. The Chowla conjecture says that for distinct h_1, ..., h_k, the sum over n <= x of lambda(n + h_1) ... lambda(n + h_k) is o(x). The case k = 1 is sum lambda(n) = o(x), which is equivalent to the prime number theorem. The case k = 2 is the statement that lambda(n) and lambda(n + h) are uncorrelated, which would follow from the Hardy-Littlewood conjectures. Tao's logarithmic version replaces the average (1/x) sum_{n<=x} by the logarithmic average (1/log x) sum_{n<=x} (1/n), which weights small n more heavily and is easier to handle. Formally: for all distinct h_1, ..., h_k, (1/x) sum_{n<=x} prod_i lambda(n + h_i) -> 0 as x -> infinity.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: For distinct integers h_1, ..., h_k, is the average of lambda(n + h_1) ... lambda(n + h_k) over n <= x equal to o(1) as x tends to infinity, where lambda is the Liouville function? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Chowla Conjecture would reshape analysis and its neighbours. The problem sits at the junction of harmonic analysis, number theory, geometry and mathematical physics, and the methods developed for it already feed into signal processing, quantum mechanics and the theory of partial differential equations. The zeta function and its relatives control the distribution of the primes, the behaviour of waves, and the spectrum of the Laplacian, so a result here propagates widely. The problem matters because it is a precise statement about a fundamental object, the Fourier transform or the zeta function, and its answer would tell us how far the classical tools of analysis can reach.

What this does NOT prove: computing the Mertens summatory function of the Mobius function, which does not establish the Chowla correlations.

The full program prints the problem statement, the status line, and the same verified computation as the demo, followed by the attempt summary. It uses only the Python standard library and runs deterministically.

The status would change only on a peer-reviewed, accepted demonstration or a verified counterexample, and neither exists as of this record.

Method

The problem is approached with the tools of analysis: estimates for exponential sums, the theory of the Fourier transform, decoupling and the polynomial method, and direct numerical computation on a finite range. For Chowla Conjecture, the analytic approach expresses the quantity of interest as an integral or a sum, splits it into a main term and an error term, and tries to bound the error term. The main term is usually understood, and the error term is the whole difficulty. Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_mertens(xs=(100, 1000, 10000)):
    def mu_upto(n):
        mu = [1] * (n + 1)
        mu[0] = 0
        primes = []
        is_comp = [False] * (n + 1)
        for i in range(2, n + 1):
            if not is_comp[i]:
                primes.append(i)
                mu[i] = -1
            for p in primes:
                if i * p > n:
                    break
                is_comp[i * p] = True
                if i % p == 0:
                    mu[i * p] = 0
                    break
                mu[i * p] = -mu[i]
        return mu
    mu = mu_upto(max(xs))
    lines = ["Mertens function check: M(x) = sum_{n<=x} mu(n)."]
    for x in xs:
        m = sum(mu[1:x + 1])
        lines.append("M(%d) = %d, |M(x)| <= sqrt(x): %s" % (x, m, abs(m) <= math.sqrt(x)))
    lines.append("Range limit: small x only; the summatory behaviour in general is the open question.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

payload = eng_mertens()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Chowla Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: computing the Mertens summatory function of the Mobius function, which does not establish the Chowla correlations")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_mertens(xs=(100, 1000, 10000)):
    def mu_upto(n):
        mu = [1] * (n + 1)
        mu[0] = 0
        primes = []
        is_comp = [False] * (n + 1)
        for i in range(2, n + 1):
            if not is_comp[i]:
                primes.append(i)
                mu[i] = -1
            for p in primes:
                if i * p > n:
                    break
                is_comp[i * p] = True
                if i % p == 0:
                    mu[i * p] = 0
                    break
                mu[i * p] = -mu[i]
        return mu
    mu = mu_upto(max(xs))
    lines = ["Mertens function check: M(x) = sum_{n<=x} mu(n)."]
    for x in xs:
        m = sum(mu[1:x + 1])
        lines.append("M(%d) = %d, |M(x)| <= sqrt(x): %s" % (x, m, abs(m) <= math.sqrt(x)))
    lines.append("Range limit: small x only; the summatory behaviour in general is the open question.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Chowla Conjecture")
    print("Edition: public")
    print("STATUS: OPEN")
    print("Statement: For distinct integers h_1, ..., h_k, is the average of lambda(n + h_1) ... lambda(n + h_k) over n <= x equal to o(1) as x tends to infinity, where lambda is the Liouville function?")
    payload = eng_mertens()
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: computing the Mertens summatory function of the Mobius function, which does not establish the Chowla correlations")
    print("Attempt summary: this public record carries no solving attempt; see the Signature mirror record.")

Elliott-Halberstam Conjecture JAH-USEQAN-000021 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The Elliott-Halberstam conjecture asserts that the primes are well distributed in arithmetic progressions up to moduli nearly as large as x itself. More precisely, it says that the primes have level of distribution 1: the error in counting primes in arithmetic progressions, summed over moduli q up to x^(1 - epsilon), is small compared with the main term. The Bombieri-Vinogradov theorem gives level 1/2 unconditionally, and Elliott-Halberstam would extend this to any level below 1. The conjecture is central because it would imply the existence of infinitely many prime pairs with gap at most 6, by the work of Goldston, Pintz and Yıldırım, and it would give strong results about primes in short intervals. It remains open, and it is one of the most important conjectures in analytic number theory.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: Do the primes have level of distribution 1 in arithmetic progressions: is the error in the prime number theorem for arithmetic progressions small on average for moduli up to x^(1 - epsilon)? The Elliott-Halberstam conjecture asserts that the primes are well distributed in arithmetic progressions up to moduli nearly as large as x itself. More precisely, it says that the primes have level of distribution 1: the error in counting primes in arithmetic progressions, summed over moduli q up to x^(1 - epsilon), is small compared with the main term. The Bombieri-Vinogradov theorem gives level 1/2 unconditionally, and Elliott-Halberstam would extend this to any level below 1. The conjecture is central because it would imply the existence of infinitely many prime pairs with gap at most 6, by the work of Goldston, Pintz and Yıldırım, and it would give strong results about primes in short intervals. It remains open, and it is one of the most important conjectures in analytic number theory.

History

Peter Elliott and Heini Halberstam stated the conjecture in 1968, in a paper on the distribution of primes in arithmetic progressions. The Bombieri-Vinogradov theorem, proved in 1965, gives level 1/2, and it was the best known result for decades. In 2013, Zhang proved a level of distribution above 1/2 for smooth moduli, which was enough to show bounded gaps between primes, and the Polymath project refined this. The full Elliott-Halberstam conjecture, with level 1 for all moduli, remains open. It is known that the conjecture would follow from the generalised Riemann hypothesis, but no unconditional argument is known. The conjecture is a benchmark for progress in the distribution of primes.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the analytic estimates needed are just beyond the reach of current methods. The main term in every approach is understood, and the error term is the whole problem: the available bounds on exponential sums, on the zeta function, and on oscillatory integrals are too weak by a small but decisive amount. Computation supports the claim to high accuracy, but a finite computation is not a demonstration, and the history of analysis contains examples where the numerical evidence was misleading. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The Bombieri-Vinogradov theorem gives level of distribution 1/2 unconditionally. Zhang's theorem gives level above 1/2 for smooth moduli, and the Polymath project improved the constants. The Elliott-Halberstam conjecture would give level 1 - epsilon for every epsilon, and it would imply bounded prime gaps of at most 6. It is also known that the conjecture is false in a certain strong form for moduli with many small prime factors, by the work of Friedlander and Granville on the distribution of primes in arithmetic progressions, but the standard form with the sum over moduli is still open. The conjecture is widely believed to be true, and it is one of the central open problems in analytic number theory.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQANS-000021 presents an attempted approach to Elliott-Halberstam Conjecture, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

Let pi(x; q, a) count primes p <= x with p = a mod q, and let E(x; q) be the maximum over a coprime to q of |pi(x; q, a) - pi(x)/phi(q)|. The Bombieri-Vinogradov theorem says sum_{q <= x^{1/2}/(log x)^A} E(x; q) << x/(log x)^A for every A. The Elliott-Halberstam conjecture says the same holds with x^{1/2} replaced by x^{1 - epsilon} for every epsilon > 0. The level of distribution is the exponent theta such that the sum is small for q <= x^theta. The GPY sieve shows that level above 1/2 gives bounded gaps, and level 1 would give gaps of at most 6. Formally: for every epsilon > 0 and every A > 0, sum_{q <= x^{1-epsilon}} E(x; q) << x/(log x)^A.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: Do the primes have level of distribution 1 in arithmetic progressions: is the error in the prime number theorem for arithmetic progressions small on average for moduli up to x^(1 - epsilon)? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Elliott-Halberstam Conjecture would reshape analysis and its neighbours. The problem sits at the junction of harmonic analysis, number theory, geometry and mathematical physics, and the methods developed for it already feed into signal processing, quantum mechanics and the theory of partial differential equations. The zeta function and its relatives control the distribution of the primes, the behaviour of waves, and the spectrum of the Laplacian, so a result here propagates widely. The problem matters because it is a precise statement about a fundamental object, the Fourier transform or the zeta function, and its answer would tell us how far the classical tools of analysis can reach.

Status

STATUS: OPEN

The problem Elliott-Halberstam Conjecture is open. It has been verified on finite ranges and studied by the methods described in this record, but no argument settles it in general. The status would change only on a peer-reviewed, accepted demonstration or a verified counterexample, and neither exists as of this record.

History

Peter Elliott and Heini Halberstam stated the conjecture in 1968, in a paper on the distribution of primes in arithmetic progressions. The Bombieri-Vinogradov theorem, proved in 1965, gives level 1/2, and it was the best known result for decades. In 2013, Zhang proved a level of distribution above 1/2 for smooth moduli, which was enough to show bounded gaps between primes, and the Polymath project refined this. The full Elliott-Halberstam conjecture, with level 1 for all moduli, remains open. It is known that the conjecture would follow from the generalised Riemann hypothesis, but no unconditional argument is known. The conjecture is a benchmark for progress in the distribution of primes.

Problem

The problem, stated exactly, is: Do the primes have level of distribution 1 in arithmetic progressions: is the error in the prime number theorem for arithmetic progressions small on average for moduli up to x^(1 - epsilon)? The Elliott-Halberstam conjecture asserts that the primes are well distributed in arithmetic progressions up to moduli nearly as large as x itself. More precisely, it says that the primes have level of distribution 1: the error in counting primes in arithmetic progressions, summed over moduli q up to x^(1 - epsilon), is small compared with the main term. The Bombieri-Vinogradov theorem gives level 1/2 unconditionally, and Elliott-Halberstam would extend this to any level below 1. The conjecture is central because it would imply the existence of infinitely many prime pairs with gap at most 6, by the work of Goldston, Pintz and Yıldırım, and it would give strong results about primes in short intervals. It remains open, and it is one of the most important conjectures in analytic number theory.

Method

The problem is approached with the tools of analysis: estimates for exponential sums, the theory of the Fourier transform, decoupling and the polynomial method, and direct numerical computation on a finite range. For Elliott-Halberstam Conjecture, the analytic approach expresses the quantity of interest as an integral or a sum, splits it into a main term and an error term, and tries to bound the error term. The main term is usually understood, and the error term is the whole difficulty. Numerical computation checks the claimed behaviour on a small range of parameters, and the demo in this record runs such a computation and reports exactly what it finds. No part of the analytic approach settles the claim in general.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_eh(qs=(3, 5, 7, 11), n=5000):
    ps = primes_upto(n)
    pi_n = len(ps)
    lines = ["Elliot-Halberstam check: primes in arithmetic progressions up to %d." % n]
    for q in qs:
        for a in range(1, q):
            if math.gcd(a, q) != 1:
                continue
            cnt = sum(1 for p in ps if p % q == a)
            lines.append("q=%d, a=%d: primes = %d, pi(n)/phi(q) = %.1f" % (q, a, cnt, pi_n / phi(q)))
    lines.append("Range limit: small moduli only; the conjecture is about moduli up to x^(1-epsilon).")
    return "\n".join(lines)

payload = eng_eh()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Elliott-Halberstam Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: comparing prime counts in arithmetic progressions with the expected share, which does not establish level of distribution 1")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Full working program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_eh(qs=(3, 5, 7, 11), n=5000):
    ps = primes_upto(n)
    pi_n = len(ps)
    lines = ["Elliot-Halberstam check: primes in arithmetic progressions up to %d." % n]
    for q in qs:
        for a in range(1, q):
            if math.gcd(a, q) != 1:
                continue

            lines.append("q=%d, a=%d: primes = %d, pi(n)/phi(q) = %.1f" % (q, a, cnt, pi_n / phi(q)))
    lines.append("Range limit: small moduli only; the conjecture is about moduli up to x^(1-epsilon).")
    return "\n".join(lines)

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Elliott-Halberstam Conjecture")
    print("Edition: public")
    print("STATUS: OPEN")
    print("Statement: Do the primes have level of distribution 1 in arithmetic progressions: is the error in the prime number theorem for arithmetic progressions small on average for moduli up to x^(1 - epsilon)?")
    payload = eng_eh()
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: comparing prime counts in arithmetic progressions with the expected share, which does not establish level of distribution 1")
    print("Attempt summary: this public record carries no solving attempt; see the Signature mirror record.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Bateman-Horn Conjecture JAH-USEQAN-000022 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The Bateman-Horn conjecture, stated by Paul Bateman and Roger Horn in 1962, gives a quantitative form of the Bunyakovsky conjecture for any finite set of polynomials. It asserts that if f_1 through f_k are irreducible integer polynomials satisfying the natural conditions, then the number of n up to x for which all f_i(n) are simultaneously prime is asymptotic to C x/(log x)^k, where C is an explicit constant computed from the local behaviour of the polynomials modulo each prime. The conjecture unifies the Hardy-Littlewood conjectures for prime tuples, the twin prime conjecture, and the Bunyakovsky conjecture, and it is the master conjecture for prime values of polynomials. It is open for every non-linear polynomial, and it is one of the central conjectures in analytic number theory.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: For irreducible integer polynomials f_1, ..., f_k satisfying the natural local conditions, is the number of n <= x with all f_i(n) prime asymptotic to C x/(log x)^k for an explicit constant C? The Bateman-Horn conjecture, stated by Paul Bateman and Roger Horn in 1962, gives a quantitative form of the Bunyakovsky conjecture for any finite set of polynomials. It asserts that if f_1 through f_k are irreducible integer polynomials satisfying the natural conditions, then the number of n up to x for which all f_i(n) are simultaneously prime is asymptotic to C x/(log x)^k, where C is an explicit constant computed from the local behaviour of the polynomials modulo each prime. The conjecture unifies the Hardy-Littlewood conjectures for prime tuples, the twin prime conjecture, and the Bunyakovsky conjecture, and it is the master conjecture for prime values of polynomials. It is open for every non-linear polynomial, and it is one of the central conjectures in analytic number theory.

History

Bateman and Horn stated the conjecture in 1962, generalising the Hardy-Littlewood conjectures for prime constellations. The conjecture was recognised as the quantitative form of the Bunyakovsky conjecture and of Schinzel's hypothesis H. In the following decades, the conjecture was verified numerically for many polynomials, and the constant C was computed in many cases. The linear case, where all polynomials have degree one, is the Dickson conjecture, which is also open but follows from the Hardy-Littlewood conjectures. The non-linear case is completely open: no polynomial of degree greater than one is known to take infinitely many prime values. The conjecture remains the standard quantitative prediction for prime values of polynomials.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the analytic estimates needed are just beyond the reach of current methods. The main term in every approach is understood, and the error term is the whole problem: the available bounds on exponential sums, on the zeta function, and on oscillatory integrals are too weak by a small but decisive amount. Computation supports the claim to high accuracy, but a finite computation is not a demonstration, and the history of analysis contains examples where the numerical evidence was misleading. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The Bateman-Horn conjecture is open in general. The constant C is explicit: C = prod_p (1 - omega(p)/p)/(1 - 1/p)^k, where omega(p) is the number of roots of the product of the polynomials modulo p. For the polynomial n^2 + 1, the constant is about 1.3727, and the count up to 10^9 matches the prediction. For twin primes, the conjecture reduces to the Hardy-Littlewood prediction with constant 2 C_2. The conjecture implies the Bunyakovsky conjecture and Schinzel's hypothesis H in quantitative form. No case with a non-linear polynomial has been settled, and the conjecture is the benchmark for progress in the theory of prime values of polynomials.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQANS-000022 presents an attempted approach to Bateman-Horn Conjecture, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

Let f_1, ..., f_k be irreducible polynomials in Z[x] with positive leading coefficients, and suppose no prime divides all values of the product f_1 ... f_k. The Bateman-Horn conjecture says the count Q(x) of n <= x with all f_i(n) prime satisfies Q(x) ~ C x/(log x)^k, where C = prod_p (1 - omega(p)/p)/(1 - 1/p)^k and omega(p) is the number of n mod p with some f_i(n) = 0 mod p. The product converges because omega(p) = k on average, by the Chebotarev density theorem. For a single polynomial f(n) = n^2 + 1, the constant is about 1.3727. The conjecture is a special case of Schinzel's hypothesis H, and it is the quantitative form of the Bunyakovsky conjecture. Formally: Q(x)/(x/(log x)^k) tends to C as x tends to infinity.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: For irreducible integer polynomials f_1, ..., f_k satisfying the natural local conditions, is the number of n <= x with all f_i(n) prime asymptotic to C x/(log x)^k for an explicit constant C? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Bateman-Horn Conjecture would reshape analysis and its neighbours. The problem sits at the junction of harmonic analysis, number theory, geometry and mathematical physics, and the methods developed for it already feed into signal processing, quantum mechanics and the theory of partial differential equations. The zeta function and its relatives control the distribution of the primes, the behaviour of waves, and the spectrum of the Laplacian, so a result here propagates widely. The problem matters because it is a precise statement about a fundamental object, the Fourier transform or the zeta function, and its answer would tell us how far the classical tools of analysis can reach.

Status

STATUS: OPEN

The problem Bateman-Horn Conjecture is open. It has been verified on finite ranges and studied by the methods described in this record, but no argument settles it in general. The status would change only on a peer-reviewed, accepted demonstration or a verified counterexample, and neither exists as of this record.

History

Bateman and Horn stated the conjecture in 1962, generalising the Hardy-Littlewood conjectures for prime constellations. The conjecture was recognised as the quantitative form of the Bunyakovsky conjecture and of Schinzel's hypothesis H. In the following decades, the conjecture was verified numerically for many polynomials, and the constant C was computed in many cases. The linear case, where all polynomials have degree one, is the Dickson conjecture, which is also open but follows from the Hardy-Littlewood conjectures. The non-linear case is completely open: no polynomial of degree greater than one is known to take infinitely many prime values. The conjecture remains the standard quantitative prediction for prime values of polynomials.

Problem

The problem, stated exactly, is: For irreducible integer polynomials f_1, ..., f_k satisfying the natural local conditions, is the number of n <= x with all f_i(n) prime asymptotic to C x/(log x)^k for an explicit constant C? The Bateman-Horn conjecture, stated by Paul Bateman and Roger Horn in 1962, gives a quantitative form of the Bunyakovsky conjecture for any finite set of polynomials. It asserts that if f_1 through f_k are irreducible integer polynomials satisfying the natural conditions, then the number of n up to x for which all f_i(n) are simultaneously prime is asymptotic to C x/(log x)^k, where C is an explicit constant computed from the local behaviour of the polynomials modulo each prime. The conjecture unifies the Hardy-Littlewood conjectures for prime tuples, the twin prime conjecture, and the Bunyakovsky conjecture, and it is the master conjecture for prime values of polynomials. It is open for every non-linear polynomial, and it is one of the central conjectures in analytic number theory.

Method

The problem is approached with the tools of analysis: estimates for exponential sums, the theory of the Fourier transform, decoupling and the polynomial method, and direct numerical computation on a finite range. For Bateman-Horn Conjecture, the analytic approach expresses the quantity of interest as an integral or a sum, splits it into a main term and an error term, and tries to bound the error term. The main term is usually understood, and the error term is the whole difficulty. Numerical computation checks the claimed behaviour on a small range of parameters, and the demo in this record runs such a computation and reports exactly what it finds. No part of the analytic approach settles the claim in general.

Independent verification of the open problem itself would require a new mathematical argument, peer-reviewed and accepted, and no such argument is claimed here.

Proof sketch

The strongest established partial result is described in the record's account of what is known, and the sketch below follows it. The Bateman-Horn conjecture is open in general. The constant C is explicit: C = prod_p (1 - omega(p)/p)/(1 - 1/p)^k, where omega(p) is the number of roots of the product of the polynomials modulo p. For the polynomial n^2 + 1, the constant is about 1.3727, and the count up to 10^9 matches the prediction. For twin primes, the conjecture reduces to the Hardy-Littlewood prediction with constant 2 C_2. The conjecture implies the Bunyakovsky conjecture and Schinzel's hypothesis H in quantitative form. No case with a non-linear polynomial has been settled, and the conjecture is the benchmark for progress in the theory of prime values of polynomials. The sketch shows the shape of the argument: a main term is identified, an error term is bounded, and the bound is too weak to reach the claim. The limits of the sketch are stated with it: the argument works on a finite range or under an unproved hypothesis, and it does not extend to the full claim. That is the honest state of the art for this problem.

Results reported

The Bateman-Horn conjecture is open in general. The constant C is explicit: C = prod_p (1 - omega(p)/p)/(1 - 1/p)^k, where omega(p) is the number of roots of the product of the polynomials modulo p. Skeptical analysis

The problem resists solution because the analytic estimates needed are just beyond the reach of current methods. The main term in every approach is understood, and the error term is the whole problem: the available bounds on exponential sums, on the zeta function, and on oscillatory integrals are too weak by a small but decisive amount. Computation supports the claim to high accuracy, but a finite computation is not a demonstration, and the history of analysis contains examples where the numerical evidence was misleading. Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_bateman_horn(n=10000):
    cnt = sum(1 for i in range(1, n + 1) if is_prime(i * i + 1))
    est = 1.3727 * math.sqrt(n) / math.log(n) * 2  # rough Hardy-Littlewood scale
    return "\n".join([
        "Bateman-Horn check: primes of the form n^2 + 1 for n <= %d." % n,
        "Primes found: %d" % cnt,
        "Rough Hardy-Littlewood scale estimate: %.1f" % est,
        "Range limit: one polynomial to %d; the conjecture covers all irreducible polynomials." % n,
    ])

payload = eng_bateman_horn()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Bateman-Horn Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: counting primes of the form n^2 + 1 for n up to 10000, which does not establish the Bateman-Horn asymptotic")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")
