import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

    est = 1.3727 * math.sqrt(n) / math.log(n) * 2  # rough Hardy-Littlewood scale
    return "\n".join([
        "Bateman-Horn check: primes of the form n^2 + 1 for n <= %d." % n,
        "Primes found: %d" % cnt,
        "Rough Hardy-Littlewood scale estimate: %.1f" % est,
        "Range limit: one polynomial to %d; the conjecture covers all irreducible polynomials." % n,
    ])

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Bateman-Horn Conjecture")
    print("Edition: public")
    print("STATUS: OPEN")
    print("Statement: For irreducible integer polynomials f_1, ..., f_k satisfying the natural local conditions, is the number of n <= x with all f_i(n) prime asymptotic to C x/(log x)^k for an explicit constant C?")
    payload = eng_bateman_horn()
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: counting primes of the form n^2 + 1 for n up to 10000, which does not establish the Bateman-Horn asymptotic")
    print("Attempt summary: this public record carries no solving attempt; see the Signature mirror record.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Sarnak Mobius Disjointness Conjecture JAH-USEQAN-000023 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} Sarnak's Möbius disjointness conjecture asserts that the Möbius function is disjoint from every deterministic dynamical system. In plain language, if (X, T) is a topological dynamical system with zero topological entropy, and f is a continuous function on X, then the average of mu(n) f(T^n x) over n up to N tends to 0 as N grows, for every starting point x. The Möbius function mu(n) is 0 when n is not squarefree and (-1)^k when n is a product of k distinct primes, so it is a pseudorandom sign. The conjecture says that this pseudorandom sign is uncorrelated with every deterministic sequence, which is the precise form of the statement that the Möbius function is random. The conjecture is open in general, although it is proved for many classes of systems.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: Is the Mobius function disjoint from every deterministic dynamical system: does the average of mu(n) f(T^n x) tend to 0 for every zero-entropy system? Sarnak's Möbius disjointness conjecture asserts that the Möbius function is disjoint from every deterministic dynamical system. In plain language, if (X, T) is a topological dynamical system with zero topological entropy, and f is a continuous function on X, then the average of mu(n) f(T^n x) over n up to N tends to 0 as N grows, for every starting point x. The Möbius function mu(n) is 0 when n is not squarefree and (-1)^k when n is a product of k distinct primes, so it is a pseudorandom sign. The conjecture says that this pseudorandom sign is uncorrelated with every deterministic sequence, which is the precise form of the statement that the Möbius function is random. The conjecture is open in general, although it is proved for many classes of systems.

History

Peter Sarnak stated the conjecture in 2010, in a lecture on the Möbius function and dynamical systems. The conjecture was motivated by the Chowla conjecture and by the philosophy that the Möbius function is pseudorandom. In the following years, the conjecture was proved for many systems: for rotations of the circle, for nilsystems, for horocycle flows, and for automatic sequences. The general case remains open, and the conjecture is now a central problem in the interaction between number theory and dynamics. The conjecture is known to follow from the Chowla conjecture, and it implies the prime number theorem in arithmetic progressions. The case of general zero-entropy systems is the open frontier.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the analytic estimates needed are just beyond the reach of current methods. The main term in every approach is understood, and the error term is the whole problem: the available bounds on exponential sums, on the zeta function, and on oscillatory integrals are too weak by a small but decisive amount. Computation supports the claim to high accuracy, but a finite computation is not a demonstration, and the history of analysis contains examples where the numerical evidence was misleading. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The Möbius disjointness conjecture is proved for rotations, nilsystems, horocycle flows, and automatic sequences, among others. The general case for arbitrary zero-entropy systems is open. The conjecture implies that the Möbius function has mean zero along every deterministic orbit, which is a strong form of pseudorandomness. It is known that the conjecture follows from the Chowla conjecture, and that it is equivalent to a statement about the Fourier coefficients of the Möbius function. The conjecture is a test case for the interaction between multiplicative number theory and ergodic theory, and it is one of the most actively studied conjectures in the area.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQANS-000023 presents an attempted approach to Sarnak Mobius Disjointness Conjecture, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

Let mu be the Möbius function, let (X, T) be a compact metric space with a homeomorphism T of zero topological entropy, and let f be continuous on X. The conjecture is that (1/N) sum_{n<=N} mu(n) f(T^n x) -> 0 as N -> infinity, for every x in X. The zero-entropy condition means the system is deterministic: the number of distinguishable orbits grows subexponentially. The conjecture says the Möbius function is orthogonal to every deterministic sequence. For a rotation of the circle, the conjecture follows from the estimate for exponential sums over the Möbius function, which is the prime number theorem in arithmetic progressions. For a general system, the conjecture would follow from the Chowla conjecture. Formally: mu is disjoint from every zero-entropy dynamical system.

The conjecture is a test case for the interaction between multiplicative number theory and ergodic theory, and it is one of the most actively studied conjectures in the area. The sketch shows the shape of the argument: a main term is identified, an error term is bounded, and the bound is too weak to reach the claim. The limits of the sketch are stated with it: the argument works on a finite range or under an unproved hypothesis, and it does not extend to the full claim. That is the honest state of the art for this problem.

Results reported

The Möbius disjointness conjecture is proved for rotations, nilsystems, horocycle flows, and automatic sequences, among others. The general case for arbitrary zero-entropy systems is open. The conjecture implies that the Möbius function has mean zero along every deterministic orbit, which is a strong form of pseudorandomness. It is known that the conjecture follows from the Chowla conjecture, and that it is equivalent to a statement about the Fourier coefficients of the Möbius function. The conjecture is a test case for the interaction between multiplicative number theory and ergodic theory, and it is one of the most actively studied conjectures in the area.

Skeptical analysis

The problem resists solution because the analytic estimates needed are just beyond the reach of current methods. The main term in every approach is understood, and the error term is the whole problem: the available bounds on exponential sums, on the zeta function, and on oscillatory integrals are too weak by a small but decisive amount. Computation supports the claim to high accuracy, but a finite computation is not a demonstration, and the history of analysis contains examples where the numerical evidence was misleading. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_mobius_corr(n=10000):
    mu = [0] * (n + 1)
    mu[1] = 1
    for i in range(2, n + 1):
        # factor i by trial division (small n)
        x, sq, cnt = i, False, 0
        p = 2
        while p * p <= x:
            if x % p == 0:
                c = 0
                while x % p == 0:
                    x //= p
                    c += 1
                if c >= 2:
                    sq = True
                cnt += 1
            p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
        if x > 1:
            cnt += 1
        mu[i] = 0 if sq else (-1 if cnt % 2 else 1)
    corr_alt = sum(mu[i] * (1 if i % 2 == 0 else -1) for i in range(1, n + 1))
    corr_per3 = sum(mu[i] * (1 if i % 3 == 0 else 0) for i in range(1, n + 1))
    return "\n".join([
        "Mobius disjointness check: correlations of mu(n) with simple sequences, n <= %d." % n,
        "Correlation with (-1)^n: %d (normalised %.4f)" % (corr_alt, corr_alt / n),
        "Correlation with the indicator of multiples of 3: %d (normalised %.4f)" % (corr_per3, corr_per3 / n),
        "Range limit: two toy sequences to %d; disjointness for all zero-entropy sequences is open." % n,
    ])

payload = eng_mobius_corr()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Sarnak Mobius Disjointness Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: computing correlations of the Mobius function with two simple periodic sequences, which does not establish disjointness for all systems")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False

            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

    mu[1] = 1
    for i in range(2, n + 1):

        x, sq, cnt = i, False, 0

        while p * p <= x:

                c = 0
                while x % p == 0:
                    x //= p
                    c += 1
                if c >= 2:
                    sq = True
                cnt += 1
            p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
        if x > 1:
            cnt += 1

    corr_per3 = sum(mu[i] * (1 if i % 3 == 0 else 0) for i in range(1, n + 1))
    return "\n".join([

        "Correlation with (-1)^n: %d (normalised %.4f)" % (corr_alt, corr_alt / n),
        "Correlation with the indicator of multiples of 3: %d (normalised %.4f)" % (corr_per3, corr_per3 / n),
        "Range limit: two toy sequences to %d; disjointness for all zero-entropy sequences is open." % n,
    ])

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Sarnak Mobius Disjointness Conjecture")
    print("Edition: public")
    print("STATUS: OPEN")
    print("Statement: Is the Mobius function disjoint from every deterministic dynamical system: does the average of mu(n) f(T^n x) tend to 0 for every zero-entropy system?")
    payload = eng_mobius_corr()
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: computing correlations of the Mobius function with two simple periodic sequences, which does not establish disjointness for all systems")
    print("Attempt summary: this public record carries no solving attempt; see the Signature mirror record.")

Local Smoothing Conjecture JAH-USEQAN-000024 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record covers {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The local smoothing conjecture, proposed by Christopher Sogge in 1991, concerns the wave equation in R^n: it asserts that solutions gain a fixed amount of regularity when averaged over a finite time interval, compared with the fixed-time estimate. More precisely, for the wave equation with initial data in a Sobolev space, the space-time norm of the solution over a time interval is smaller than the fixed-time norm by a factor that reflects the smoothing. The conjecture is the strongest in a sequence of conjectures in harmonic analysis: it implies the Bochner-Riesz conjecture, which implies the restriction conjecture, which implies the Kakeya conjecture. The conjecture was proved in dimension 2 by Guth, Wang and Zhang in 2019, and it remains open in dimensions 3 and above. In 2026 a preprint claimed a demonstration, which has not been peer-reviewed as of the record's writing, so the problem is still open.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: Do solutions of the wave equation gain regularity when averaged in time: is the local smoothing estimate true for all exponents allowed by the Knapp example? The local smoothing conjecture, proposed by Christopher Sogge in 1991, concerns the wave equation in R^n: it asserts that solutions gain a fixed amount of regularity when averaged over a finite time interval, compared with the fixed-time estimate. More precisely, for the wave equation with initial data in a Sobolev space, the space-time norm of the solution over a time interval is smaller than the fixed-time norm by a factor that reflects the smoothing. The conjecture is the strongest in a sequence of conjectures in harmonic analysis: it implies the Bochner-Riesz conjecture, which implies the restriction conjecture, which implies the Kakeya conjecture. The conjecture was proved in dimension 2 by Guth, Wang and Zhang in 2019, and it remains open in dimensions 3 and above. In 2026 a preprint claimed a demonstration, which has not been peer-reviewed as of the record's writing, so the problem is still open.

History

Sogge proposed the conjecture in 1991, in a paper on the propagation of singularities and maximal functions. The conjecture became a central problem in harmonic analysis, because it sits at the top of a tower of conjectures: local smoothing implies Bochner-Riesz, which implies restriction, which implies Kakeya. In 2019, Guth, Wang and Zhang proved the conjecture in dimension 2, using the polynomial method and decoupling. The higher-dimensional case remains open, and it is the subject of active research. In 2026, a preprint appeared claiming a demonstration of the conjecture; as of the record's writing it has not been peer-reviewed, and the community has not accepted it, so the conjecture is still regarded as open pending verification.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the analytic estimates needed are just beyond the reach of current methods. The main term in every approach is understood, and the error term is the whole problem: the available bounds on exponential sums, on the zeta function, and on oscillatory integrals are too weak by a small but decisive amount. Computation supports the claim to high accuracy, but a finite computation is not a demonstration, and the history of analysis contains examples where the numerical evidence was misleading. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a finite range and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The local smoothing conjecture is proved in dimension 2 and open in dimensions 3 and above. The conjecture implies the Bochner-Riesz conjecture and the restriction conjecture, and it is the strongest of the three. A 2026 preprint claiming a demonstration has not been peer-reviewed and is not accepted as a resolution. The best known results in higher dimensions are partial: the conjecture is known for some exponents, and the full range of exponents is open. The problem is connected with the Kakeya problem and with the theory of Fourier integral operators, and it is one of the central open problems in harmonic analysis.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQANS-000024 presents an attempted approach to Local Smoothing Conjecture, labelled there as an unverified attempt, and nothing in that record is a verified solution. The public record states the problem, its history, and what is known, and it does not claim a solution.

Math Proof

For the wave equation u_tt - Delta u = 0 in R^n with initial data (f, 0), the solution is u(t) = cos(t sqrt(-Delta)) f. The fixed-time estimate says the L^p norm of u(t) is controlled by the Sobolev norm of f, with a loss of derivatives. The local smoothing conjecture says that the space-time norm (int_0^1 ||u(t)||_{L^p}^p dt)^{1/p} is controlled by a weaker Sobolev norm, with a gain of 1/p derivatives in the optimal case. The conjecture is that for p > 2n/(n-1), the estimate holds with the sharp regularity. The Knapp example, a wave packet concentrated in a small cap of the light cone, shows the exponent is best possible. The conjecture is proved in dimension 2 by decoupling, and the higher-dimensional case needs a stronger form of decoupling. Formally: the local smoothing estimate holds for all p in the sharp range.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: Do solutions of the wave equation gain regularity when averaged in time: is the local smoothing estimate true for all exponents allowed by the Knapp example? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Local Smoothing Conjecture would reshape analysis and its neighbours. The problem sits at the junction of harmonic analysis, number theory, geometry and mathematical physics, and the methods developed for it already feed into signal processing, quantum mechanics and the theory of partial differential equations. The zeta function and its relatives control the distribution of the primes, the behaviour of waves, and the spectrum of the Laplacian, so a result here propagates widely. The problem matters because it is a precise statement about a fundamental object, the Fourier transform or the zeta function, and its answer would tell us how far the classical tools of analysis can reach.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_smoothing(n=64, steps=50):
    # Free Schrodinger equation on a periodic grid, explicit finite differences,
    # complex arithmetic; L2 norm should be conserved.
    import cmath
    dx = 1.0 / n
    dt = 0.00005
    psi = [cmath.exp(2j * math.pi * j / n) * math.exp(-((j - n // 2) ** 2) / 50.0) for j in range(n)]
    def norm2(v):
        return sum(abs(x) ** 2 for x in v) * dx
    n0 = norm2(psi)
    for _ in range(steps):
        new = []
        for j in range(n):
            lap = (psi[(j - 1) % n] - 2 * psi[j] + psi[(j + 1) % n]) / (dx * dx)
            new.append(psi[j] + 1j * dt * lap / 2.0)
        psi = new
    n1 = norm2(psi)
    return "\n".join([
        "Local smoothing check: free Schrodinger evolution on a periodic grid of %d points, %d steps." % (n, steps),
        "Initial L2 norm squared: %.10f" % n0,
        "Final L2 norm squared: %.10f" % n1,
        "Relative change: %.3e" % (abs(n1 - n0) / n0),
        "Range limit: a small numerical evolution; local smoothing estimates are about all solutions.",
    ])

payload = eng_smoothing()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Local Smoothing Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: evolving the free Schrodinger equation on a small periodic grid and checking norm behaviour, which does not establish local smoothing estimates")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)

Full working program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_smoothing(n=64, steps=50):
    # Free Schrodinger equation on a periodic grid, explicit finite differences,
    # complex arithmetic; L2 norm should be conserved.
    import cmath
    dx = 1.0 / n
    dt = 0.00005
    psi = [cmath.exp(2j * math.pi * j / n) * math.exp(-((j - n // 2) ** 2) / 50.0) for j in range(n)]
    def norm2(v):
        return sum(abs(x) ** 2 for x in v) * dx
    n0 = norm2(psi)
    for _ in range(steps):
        new = []
        for j in range(n):
            lap = (psi[(j - 1) % n] - 2 * psi[j] + psi[(j + 1) % n]) / (dx * dx)
            new.append(psi[j] + 1j * dt * lap / 2.0)
        psi = new
    n1 = norm2(psi)
    return "\n".join([
        "Local smoothing check: free Schrodinger evolution on a periodic grid of %d points, %d steps." % (n, steps),
        "Initial L2 norm squared: %.10f" % n0,
        "Final L2 norm squared: %.10f" % n1,
        "Relative change: %.3e" % (abs(n1 - n0) / n0),
        "Range limit: a small numerical evolution; local smoothing estimates are about all solutions.",
    ])

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Local Smoothing Conjecture")
    print("Edition: public")
    print("STATUS: OPEN")
    print("Statement: Do solutions of the wave equation gain regularity when averaged in time: is the local smoothing estimate true for all exponents allowed by the Knapp example?")
    payload = eng_smoothing()
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: evolving the free Schrodinger equation on a small periodic grid and checking norm behaviour, which does not establish local smoothing estimates")
    print("Attempt summary: this public record carries no solving attempt; see the Signature mirror record.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
